# Decisions — the ADR record

An **ADR** here is not a memo. It is the durable record of a `design → red-team → prove → judge`
loop: competing designs implemented in real C++23, attacked adversarially, compiled under multiple
compilers, run under sanitizers, and measured — before a judge picks a winner on evidence.

This is the process Quark uses (36 ADRs and counting), and it exists because the alternative —
settling a hot-path or security-critical question by argument — produces designs that read well and
fail under load or attack.

## When an ADR is required

- Any **security-critical** choice: capability representation, isolation boundary, taint mechanism,
  approval binding, secret handling.
- Any **hot-path** choice measured by a 023 budget.
- Any choice where **credible designs disagree** and the disagreement is not resolvable by reading.
- Any **promotion** of an RFC from Draft to Proven — the gate execution *is* the ADR.

Ordinary implementation choices do not need one. An ADR for everything is an ADR for nothing.

## What an ADR must contain

1. **The question**, stated so that it has a wrong answer.
2. **The competing designs**, each steelmanned.
3. **Falsifiable claims** per design — specifically the fast/safe/correct claims, each paired with
   an experiment that would *disprove* it.
4. **The red-team attack** on each design, hardest where it claims to be fast or safe.
5. **The executed evidence**: commands, compilers, sanitizers, measured numbers (percentiles, not
   means), with **positive controls** — a test that cannot fail proves nothing, and for security
   claims this is mandatory (022 §5).
6. **Per-claim verdicts**: CORRECT / WRONG / INCONCLUSIVE, decided by observed output, not argument.
   `INCONCLUSIVE` is an honest verdict and must not be laundered into either of the others.
7. **The decision**, the specs it binds, and the residual risks or deferred gates.

## Naming

`ADR-NNN-<kebab-case-question>.md`, numbered sequentially and permanently. A superseded ADR is
marked Superseded and points forward; it is never deleted, because the reasoning that was wrong is
part of the record.

**That protection is for decisions, not for documents that were never decisions.** A file that turns
out not to meet "What an ADR must contain" — no competing designs, nothing implemented, no judge
picking a winner — is **removed from this directory outright**, and whatever it established moves to
the home the project already has for it: a toolchain or protocol claim to `docs/research/<date>-<topic>.md`
(CLAUDE.md), a reproducer to `tests/experiments/`, an enacted change to a comment at the site it
governs. Git history keeps the text; the index keeps its meaning. Retiring the number rather than
reusing it is enough — a decisions/ directory padded with documents that are not decisions is worth
less than a smaller one that is all signal, and a bug or a bad call gets reworked rather than
enshrined.

## Index

| ADR | Question | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| [001](ADR-001-shellrunner-grammar-and-dispatch.md) | How does `ShellRunner` parse and dispatch without a name ever resolving to an arbitrary process, and with the parser itself safe against adversarial input? | **Judged.** Design A (recursive-descent → AST → tree-walking eval) accepted, scoped to Windows/MSVC+clang; Design B never implemented (not defeated on evidence). Partial evidence toward 010 §9 G2/G3/G4; G6 (cross-platform) and others remain open. |
| [002](ADR-002-pythonrunner-embedding-and-mediation.md) | How does embedded CPython enforce a closed import allowlist and mediate `open`/`socket`/`subprocess`, robustly rather than as a degrading blocklist? | **Judged.** Meta-path finder mechanism accepted; "closed by construction" narrowed after prove phase found granting numpy+pandas requires ~130 allowlisted names including `ctypes`/`winreg`/`subprocess` (module-name granularity can't distinguish trusted internals from guest code). See OpenQuestions.md for the caller-aware-gating follow-up. **§11 addendum (2026-08-11), Proposed, awaiting Judged**: §6 item 5 (subinterpreter pooling) answered NO on this build — `Py_NewInterpreterFromConfig` itself works, but `import numpy` fails under both the strict PEP 684 config and the legacy shared-GIL config against the installed numpy 2.3.3, and the strict config's failure crashes the process on teardown (`STATUS_HEAP_CORRUPTION`) — a real, reproduced, process-crash-capable hazard, not merely an incompatibility. |
| [003](ADR-003-caller-aware-import-gating.md) | How does the import gate distinguish a granted package's own internal use of a sensitive module from guest code importing the same module directly (OQ-15)? | **Judged.** Host-side dual-registry + C-level frame-stack-walk mechanism accepted, narrowing ADR-002's scope-limitation for `ctypes`/`_ctypes`/`winreg`/`_wmi`/`_winapi`/`subprocess`. Not airtight (gadget-chaining and C-reentrancy questions remain open); one real entry-point gap (`importlib.import_module` on a cache miss) was found and fixed during independent re-verification, underscoring that this class of design has a demonstrated history of missed entry points. Resolves OQ-15 for the measured target. |
| [004](ADR-004-appcontainer-native-jail-windows-backend.md) | Does AppContainer + Job Object satisfy `native-jail`'s Windows contract across pure-Python, native-extension, and CodeAct-authored guest code? | **Judged (2026-08-23; was "Spiked, not Judged" from 2026-08-02).** AppContainer (zero capabilities + `CHILD_PROCESS_RESTRICTED`) confirmed correct for process/network denial and all three CodeAct library tiers against a vendored CPython 3.14. Headline finding: AppContainer's ACL model is **not** a sufficient filesystem boundary — `win.ini`/`hosts` remain readable via Windows' own inherited `ALL (RESTRICTED) APPLICATION PACKAGES` grants regardless of configuration, confirming 008 §1b's choice to make interpreter-level `open()` mediation primary. **§10 addendum (built and measured, same day):** `JobObjectLimits` — memory/active-process limits precise and reliable; `cpu_ms`/`JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_JOB_TIME` fired in only 3/11 measured runs and must be treated as best-effort, `wall_ms` is the real enforcement point. **§11/§12 (2026-08-23): the missing independent red-team pass, run for real, plus the LPAC decision, both closed.** LPAC vs. regular AppContainer: real execution (`tests/adr004_lpac_decision_spike.cpp`, new) shows LPAC does NOT close the win.ini/hosts finding — stays with regular AppContainer. The red-team pass (a fresh reviewer, no prior context) found and this same pass FIXED: a **BLOCKING** finding, verified by execution — `exec()`'s `CreateProcessW` had no `PROC_THREAD_ATTRIBUTE_HANDLE_LIST`, so with `bInheritHandles=TRUE` a zero-capability AppContainer child could inherit and read ANY other inheritable handle open in the host process, reproduced directly (a probe child read an unrelated host secret through an inherited handle) — falsifying the AC-S1 "no outbound socket" claim whenever the host had any other inheritable handle open (realistic, since host code owns networking in-process). Fixed: `exec()` now uses the same 3-entry `HANDLE_LIST` pattern `create_python_worker()` already had. Also fixed in the same pass: `exec()`'s `hStdInput` was the host's own real stdin with no capability grant (now an explicit empty `NUL` handle); `create_python_worker()` leaked 4 HANDLEs on every failure path after worker-process creation (new `close_worker_handles()` helper, wired into `destroy()` and all 4 failure returns); `create_python_worker()` re-granted the same deployment-fixed ACL paths on every session, growing the shared profile's DACL without bound (new `grant_ro_deduped()`, mirrors `shared_profile()`'s own reuse model). Re-verified: full rebuild, 255/261 passing (the 6 failures are the pre-existing, already-disclosed ADR-081 Slice-2 regression, confirmed unrelated by `git stash`-testing the pre-fix code), the native-jail-specific suite 11/11. |
| [005](ADR-005-capability-bearer-tokens-cross-process.md) | Should a capability that must cross a process boundary be a self-verifying macaroon-style bearer token, or an opaque reference checked against a host-side registry? | **Judged, narrowly.** Resolves OQ-3. Bearer token (`trust/capability_token.hpp`) accepted for `ExpiresAt`/`PathPrefix` caveats — proven attenuation-only and forge-resistant under red-team (bit-flip, field tamper, caveat-strip, reorder, fabricated-parent derivation all rejected; zero ASan findings, UBSan not attempted — no clang toolchain on this machine), no round-trip required by construction. Host-side registry (`trust/capability_registry.hpp`) not rejected — it is the answer for capabilities needing immediate revocation, a gap the token structurally has. Performance claim left **INCONCLUSIVE**: measured local cost favored the registry, confounded by an unoptimized (uncached) BCrypt handle in this pass and by the registry benchmark omitting the real cross-process round-trip Design A avoids by construction. **Residual (noted 2026-08-23, component-role-audit-tracker.md Finding M):** both designs are real and tested, but neither has a production consumer yet — zero non-test `#include` of either header anywhere in `include/`/`src/`, including the exact boundaries their own comments name as the reason to exist (the `remote` sandbox profile, delegated A2A calls, a remote plugin). "Judged" here means the primitives are sound, not that anything is wired to them. |
| [006](ADR-006-agent-spawn-depth-budget-bound.md) | Are depth and budget bounds sufficient to contain `agent.spawn`'s recursion hazard, and how is the bound represented so guest code cannot widen it? | **Judged (partial — depth only).** Partially resolves 026 §9 Q1 / OQ-14. `SpawnBudget` (`trust/spawn_budget.hpp`) — a private-construction, strictly-decrementing in-process value type, not a cryptographic token (agent.spawn never crosses an OS process boundary) — proven exhaustive over 51 starting depths plus two compile-time `static_assert`s blocking out-of-class construction; zero ASan findings. Depth bounds confirmed sufficient against unbounded recursion, **conditional on** the effect-mediation boundary (006 §9 G4) holding, which this ADR assumes rather than re-proves. Cost bounds (wall-clock/token spend per spawn) remain open, tracked against 023. |
| [007](ADR-007-span-level-taint-vs-per-item.md) | Should taint be tracked at sub-string (span) granularity rather than per-`Content`-item? | **Judged — rejected.** Resolves OQ-5. A span-taint prototype confirmed the precision claim (per-item taint really does deny genuinely trusted material sharing an item with tainted material) but red-team found a naive, otherwise-plausible `concat` implementation silently under-taints real danger bytes on the first attempt — a new security-relevant bug class per-item taint structurally cannot have. The concrete precision gap is already closed today by keeping trusted/tainted material as separate `ContentItem`s (017 §3's existing structural-separation idiom), at the same precision, without that risk. Decision: keep per-item taint (003 §2); do not build span-level taint. |
| [008](ADR-008-wasm3-cold-start-vs-wasmtime.md) | Does wasm3's cold-start advantage over Wasmtime, measured on our own workload, clear the 023-budget bar OQ-12 set before it is even a candidate? | **Judged — the gate clears, the runtime decision is deferred.** Resolves OQ-12. Real, symmetric, correctness-checked cold-start measurement (parse/compile+instantiate+call+teardown, trivial exported function, 1000 iterations, four consistent runs): wasm3 p50 ≈ 0.7 µs vs. Wasmtime p50 ≈ 111-128 µs (160-180x), p99 ≈ 0.7-1.0 µs vs. 241-289 µs (250-400x) — a real, structural gap (interpreter vs. JIT compilation), not measurement noise. 008 §1a's rejection of wasm3 as the *primary* runtime (Component Model/WASI 0.3 support) is unaffected and unchanged. Whether to actually build a scoped second runtime behind the same host interface is left as a separate, larger decision this ADR does not make — it did not evaluate 008 §1a's named cost ("two sandbox-escape surfaces and two conformance stories"), and Wasmtime's realistic cached/precompiled deployment shape (which would narrow the gap) was not measured. |
| [009](ADR-009-capability-set-enforcement-mechanism.md) | How should in-process `Capability`/`CapabilitySet` be represented and enforced so that empty-by-default, attenuation-only, and per-invocation revocation are structural properties, not conventions? | **Judged.** Resolves 007 §3's in-process enforcement half (Milestone 2, Phase A). Parameterized `cap::` variant (16 kinds) + checked `attenuate()` (narrowing-only, all-or-nothing) + shared-ticket `BoundCapability`/`revoke()` accepted; a whole-set epoch-counter alternative rejected by design-level reasoning alone (fails 006 §8 G3 — an ordinary call doesn't bump a macro-level epoch, so a stashed handle would survive to the next call). Proven via a positive-control-bearing suite (six independent widening axes rejected, a stashed-copy handle confirmed dead after revocation, an unrelated handle confirmed unaffected) on Windows (MSVC + ASan, zero findings) and Linux (Docker, gcc-14) — 27/27 checks on both. A real `agentengine::ToolCall` naming collision (capability vs. content.hpp's message-content type) was found via a live build failure and fixed by namespacing the per-kind structs under `agentengine::cap`. |
| [010](ADR-010-wasm-component-host-manifest-capability-binding.md) | How does the `wasm` `SandboxBackend` bind a component's manifest-declared capability requirements to real, checked `Capability` grants at load time? | **Judged.** See the ADR for its own question/decision detail (M2 Phase D). |
| [011](ADR-011-first-party-egress-proxy.md) | How does a first-party egress proxy (008 §10 Q3) mediate outbound HTTP for every sandbox profile without a bypassable boundary? | **Judged.** `HostEgressProxy` (`NetEgressBackend` concept) accepted: resolve-once-connect-to-verified-IPv4-literal closes DNS-rebinding by construction; a blocked-range table checked on the binary address is immune to encoding-bypass classes by construction; CRLF-injection gate; byte cap enforced mid-stream; no redirect-following. Plain HTTP and IPv4 only this milestone, both named explicitly. Verified Windows/Linux with G2-style positive controls (`tests/test_net_egress_proxy.cpp`); a real TCP RST/`ECONNRESET` bug was found and fixed in the test double, not the product code. **Addendum (2026-08-19):** the §9 residual "chunked transfer-encoding is not supported" was itself wrong — a chunked, no-`Content-Length` response doesn't time out (every request already sends `Connection: close`), it silently corrupts `resp.body` with raw chunk framing bytes. Fixed with a real `dechunk_body()`, applied AFTER `parse_http_response()` (not inside it — that broke the streaming path's header-only call on the first attempt, caught by the full suite and fixed same day) in the two buffered exchange functions only; chunk extensions/trailers discarded, malformed framing fails closed with `net.protocol_error`; no change to the read loop's own byte-cap-during-the-loop discipline (claim C8). This also surfaced and fixed a second real bug: `protocol/openai` and `protocol/anthropic`'s `chat()` each carried their own now-redundant chunked-decode workaround that, once the transport layer started dechunking first, ran a second time on already-plain JSON and misparsed it — both retired in favor of trusting `resp->body`. Neither provider's streaming path was touched. Full suite 195/195, zero regressions, both rounds. Bug fix against this ADR's own already-decided design, not a new ADR (ADR-062 removal precedent). |
| [012](ADR-012-sandbox-profile-template-parameter-kind.md) | What kind of thing is `SandboxProfile<P>`'s template parameter `P`: an enum value naming a closed set of engine profiles, or any type satisfying `SandboxBackend`? | **Judged.** `P` is any type satisfying the new `SandboxProfileArg` concept — a real `SandboxBackend`, or the resolution selector `Strict` (replacing the independently-broken, undefined `Profile::Strict`) — never an enum value; 008 §2a's extensibility promise made true by construction. A non-conforming `P` fails to compile at the declaration site, proven by a `try_compile()` gate matching the existing 007 §9 G1/G2 pattern, plus a runtime round-trip test. Both RFCs (002 §2/§3, 008 §3) corrected to match. `check_sandbox_profile_availability` is now real for the resolved cases; `Strict`'s resolution against real backend availability, and the separate per-tool backend-compatibility check, remain deferred — both need machinery (an Engine-level registry, a new policy tag) this ADR did not build as a drive-by. |
| [013](ADR-013-https-egress-tls-client.md) | Where does the egress proxy's TLS client come from, and how is the dependency vendored, given 008 §10 Q3 draws the "needs a vetted crypto library" line at TLS specifically? | **Judged.** mbedTLS vendored directly (3.6.7, exact-pinned + checksummed via `FetchContent`, this project's own established discipline — not Quark's laxer vcpkg-floor `QUARK_WITH_MBEDTLS` path), behind `AGENTENGINE_WITH_HTTPS` (default off). `TlsClientSession` provides real system-CA-style validation (a separately-pinned, compiled-in CA bundle) and real hostname verification, unlike Quark's `SecureTransport` (deliberately nulled-out hostname check for cluster mTLS, re-confirmed unsuitable). Platform-native TLS (Windows SChannel / Linux OpenSSL) was considered and rejected per explicit user direction, trading a bundled CA file's maintenance cost for one portable code path. Proven with in-memory-generated test certificates against a real loopback TLS server: valid-cert positive control, untrusted-root/hostname-mismatch/expired-cert rejection all confirmed on Windows and Linux. A real Quark header bug (missing `<atomic>` include, Linux-only) was found and fixed on this project's own side (Quark never patched in-tree). Default (HTTPS-off) build/test posture reconfirmed fully unaffected. |
| [014](ADR-014-worktree-mount-path-canonicalization.md) | How does a real, OS-backed materialization of a worktree mount resolve a guest-supplied relative path so that `..`, absolute redirects, symlink/junction/reparse-point crossing, ADS, `\\?\` prefixes, unicode-normalization tricks, and TOCTOU re-resolution can never name anything outside the mount root? | **Judged.** Design A (lexical canonicalize-then-string-check, reopen the checked string later) rejected: a real, deterministically-reproduced TOCTOU proof shows the reopened handle reads content that was swapped in AFTER the check passed. Design B (`open_within_mount_root`, `core/worktree_mount_fs.hpp`) accepted: a single handle-based `CreateFileW` lets Windows resolve reparse points transparently, and containment is verified from the resulting HANDLE (`GetFinalPathNameByHandleW`), never from a re-parsed string — proven immune to the identical interleaving (an already-open handle is unaffected by a later swap; a fresh post-swap request re-resolves and is correctly rejected). A real production bug (missing `FILE_SHARE_DELETE` on the target handle, which would let an open guest handle block a host-side delete/replace) was found and fixed during the same prove pass. 22/22 checks, Windows only — 8.3 short-name aliasing and real-symlink-vs-junction parity remain named, untested residuals on Windows. **Addendum (2026-08-06, Phase C4):** Linux parity closed as an ordinary follow-on task (no second ADR) — `open()` + `readlink("/proc/self/fd/N")` (`core/worktree_mount_fs_posix.hpp`/`.cpp`) ports the identical "verify from the object opened, not a re-parsed string" property; 21/21 checks on real, unprivileged Linux filesystem I/O, including the same TOCTOU proof via real symlinks. |
| [015](ADR-015-shellrunner-grammar-parser-fuzzing.md) | How does `MediatedShellRunner`'s grammar parser get continuous, corpus-driven fuzzing under ASan/UBSan, gated in CI, given 010 §9 G8 requires it (and ADR-001's own fixed-corpus proof stopped short of it)? | **Judged.** A real libFuzzer harness (`tests/fuzz/mediated_shell_parser_fuzz.cpp`) against the pure `parse()` entry point, Clang-only (`AGENTENGINE_BUILD_SHELL_FUZZER`, default off), seeded from a checked-in corpus, run 120s/push in a new `windows-shell-fuzz` CI job. Two real Windows libFuzzer ABI mismatches (CRT static/dynamic, MSVC STL ASan container-annotation flag) found and fixed during setup. The harness's teeth were proven directly: ADR-001 finding 12's nesting-depth guard was deliberately reintroduced-as-disabled, produced a real native stack-overflow crash the harness correctly detected via both direct replay and its own corpus-driven crash path, then the fix was restored and reconfirmed clean (23,517 executions, 0 findings). AFL++ (Windows support too weak) and extending the existing fixed-corpus approach (not what G8 asks for) both considered and rejected. Residuals named: the CI job was reviewed but not executed on an actual runner (no configured remote); no semantic oracle (crashes/hangs/UB only, not silent wrong-output bugs); no corpus persistence across CI runs; Linux and the full dispatch/evaluate path both out of scope. |
| [016](ADR-016-provider-egress-address-policy.md) | Should host-initiated provider egress (`perform_provider_https_exchange`) reuse ADR-011's guest-path `resolve_and_validate` blocked-range filter, given the threat model differs (host-authored URLs, not guest-supplied)? | **Accepted** (2026-08-07). Split into `resolve_and_validate(host, port)` (blocked-range filter, WASM guest egress only) and `resolve_host(host, port)` (no filter, host-initiated provider calls) over one shared resolution implementation, so the two cannot drift. Both keep ADR-011's resolve-once-connect-to-a-verified-literal DNS-rebinding defense unchanged. Supersedes the Milestone 5 Phase C implementation note that reused the guest filter "as defense-in-depth even though the threat model differs." |
| [017](ADR-017-stream-consumer-cancellation-signal.md) | How does a stream consumer's cancellation propagate into a provider stream's blocking I/O, closing 004 §7 G2's "no orphaned socket" gate? | **Accepted** (2026-08-07). `make_stream<T>` constructs one `std::stop_source`, copied (not duplicated — shared stop-state) to both the `stream<T>` consumer half and the `stream_producer<T>` half; `cancel()`/destructor request stop, `stream_producer<T>::stop_token()` hands the matching token to `perform_provider_https_exchange`, reaching the mid-flight cancellation Phase C2 already proved at the raw exchange layer. Closes the Milestone 5 Phase J3 residual. |
| [018](ADR-018-agent-session-real-chat-client-wiring.md) | Can `AgentSession<ChatClientT>` host a non-default-constructible real `ChatClient` conformer without breaking every existing default-constructible mock conformer? | **Accepted** (2026-08-07). `chat_client_` becomes `std::optional<ChatClientT>`; every pre-existing conformer is bit-for-bit unaffected (still default-constructible, engaged at construction exactly as the value member was); `emplace_chat_client(Args&&...)` constructs a non-default-constructible client in place after the actor exists. Closes the Milestone 5 Phase J1 residual. |
| [019](ADR-019-incremental-streaming-response.md) | How does a provider response stream incrementally (SSE/chunked-transfer) into `chat_stream()` without baking one vendor's event dialect into shared framing? | **Accepted** (2026-08-07). Three independently-testable layers: stateful pure `ChunkedBodyDecoder`/`SseEventFramer` framing decoders (`sandbox/incremental_http_body.hpp`) that return raw event blocks, not parsed fields, so OpenAI's `data:`-only and Anthropic's `data:`+`event:` dialects both read the same blocks; a streaming transport pair (`perform_http/https_exchange_streaming`) identical to the buffering pair (same byte-cap, no-redirect, `stop_token` cancellation) except body bytes reach an `on_body` sink as they arrive; real Phase D/E backend `chat_stream()` bodies wired to both. Closes the Milestone 5 residual naming this gap in both backends' file banners. |
| [020](ADR-020-portable-reasoning-effort.md) | Should `reasoning_effort` be a portable, backend-independent knob on `ChatRequest`, and is `prompt_cache_key` ready to design alongside it? | **Accepted** (2026-08-07). `enum class reasoning_effort {off, low, medium, high}` as `std::optional<reasoning_effort>`, appended last on `ChatRequest` (003 §6's field-ordering lesson). Chosen over a per-backend constructor field because live-probe evidence showed a real, measurable effect on one backend and none on the other — an agent author needing backend-specific knowledge to "reason harder" was judged worse than the asymmetry. `prompt_cache_key` stays deferred (evidence insufficient to design it yet). Amends 004-Model-Provider-Plane.md §2. |
| [021](ADR-021-inbound-protocol-trust-boundary.md) | Does AgentEngine's inbound MCP/A2A surface terminate TLS+OAuth-2.1-style auth itself (first-party), or defer to an operator-supplied reverse proxy trusting a forwarded, signed principal header? | **Superseded by [039](ADR-039-inbound-transport-host-pluggable.md)'s strategic direction; §7's bearer-token mechanism survives unchanged.** Historical text kept below. Was: **Judged, strategic question only.** Design A (first-party) accepted; Design B (proxy-delegated) not rejected but found to need a structural fix (per-request signed binding) before it's safe, after red-team surfaced HTTP request-smuggling as a principal-forgery/cross-tenant-leak primitive specific to its pooled-connection trust model — a severe gap the original claims table didn't cover. Design C (extend Quark's `TcpTransport`) was too-hastily rejected in the original design pass; Quark's own sanctioned `event_loop()` extension point (already used by `VoiceChannel`/ADR-030) reopens a real "share the reactor, don't touch the wire protocol" option for whichever design's listener gets built. Design A's core cryptographic mechanism is fully proven: `trust/hmac.hpp` (extracted, shared HMAC-SHA256/constant-time-compare primitive, also now used by ADR-005's own capability token) and `trust/bearer_token.hpp` (mint/verify with algorithm pinned by construction — no alg/kid field exists to confuse — audience/issuer validated against caller-supplied config never token content, and a bounded, `exp`-pruned replay guard with its single-instance limitation honestly demonstrated rather than hidden). 32/32 checks including a mandatory positive control. The network listener, server-role TLS, and HTTP/1.1 request-parsing/smuggling-hardening remain real, named follow-on work (each plausibly its own ADR) — Milestone 7 Phase C4+/D5+ stay blocked on that, not on this ADR's own now-settled question. |
| [022](ADR-022-inbound-listener-reactor-model.md) | Does the future inbound HTTP listener (ADR-021 follow-on) share Quark's own `TcpTransport::event_loop()` reactor, or run a fully standalone one? | **Superseded in full by [039](ADR-039-inbound-transport-host-pluggable.md)** — no first-party listener means no reactor question; also moot since ADR-037 deleted the Quark types both designs were built from. Historical text kept below. Was: **Judged.** Design B (standalone reactor) accepted over Design A (shared reactor) and Design C (hybrid accept-then-handoff, rejected outright). Red-team found the shared-reactor starvation risk is not speculative — `voice_channel.hpp`'s own header cites a measured, already-executed negative control (ADR-030's F2r) proving an unbudgeted consumer fully starves the shared loop's other fds at P>=64; this ADR reuses that evidence directly rather than re-measuring it, the same "cite proven evidence" move ADR-021 made reusing ADR-005's HMAC primitive. Design A remains implementable but only under a binding, VoiceChannel-shaped budget discipline that must never regress, with a strictly worse failure MODE for the same class of implementer bug (a loop-thread-contract violation in Design A can corrupt the whole node's cluster-transport state; the equivalent bug in Design B is contained to the HTTP reactor's own maps) — plus a real, previously-unflagged Windows-vs-Linux `add_fd` asymmetry (silent overwrite on Windows, loud rejection on Linux) that breaks any double-registration-guard assumption on this project's primary dev platform. Design B's own claimed isolation was narrowed after red-team found it doesn't cover fd-table exhaustion, heap pressure, or CPU-scheduler contention — real residuals, named, with this project's existing Job-Object-limit tooling (ADR-004) flagged as the applicable mitigation when the listener is actually built. A pre-existing, out-of-this-ADR's-scope gap was found and recorded rather than lost: Quark's own `TcpTransport::on_accept_ready()` accepts any TCP connection with zero authentication before registering it on the shared reactor, meaning the starvation attack this ADR analyzes for HTTP traffic is already possible against Quark's own cluster port today, gated only by network topology. No new measured benchmark code was written — the mechanism in question is unchanged from `VoiceChannel`'s already-measured context, so re-measuring it would reproduce a known result rather than test anything new. |
| [023](ADR-023-response-format-codec-seam.md) | How does AgentEngine extract reasoning/tool-call content from a raw, non-JSON-field model response format (Harmony, DeepSeek/Hermes/Qwen delimiters, `<think>` tags) extensibly, without a text-derived reconstruction gaining a vendor-structured call's unconditional authority? | **Judged (2026-08-10, both phases implemented).** A declarative grammar table (`static constexpr` POD, no regex/code) proven, by independently-reverified execution, to correctly extract tool calls from real Harmony/DeepSeek/Hermes/Qwen example strings. Red-team found the naive "scan only at stream boundaries" mitigation fails against a laundering attack (a model quoting attacker-controlled tool-result text that contains valid call syntax) and that schema-only auto-declassification is unconditionally unsafe (invisible to existing taint-based policy); accepted instead a capability-scoped declassifier. **Shipped, both halves:** (1) `include/agentengine/core/response_format_codec.hpp`, wired into `OpenAIChatClient` behind an off-by-default `scan_response_format_leaks` flag — Reasoning/`<think>`-channel extraction for UI. (2) A real `007 §4` amendment (`call_provenance`) plus `invoke_tool` step 5 rewired (`core/tool_pipeline.hpp`) so a `text_derived` call auto-declassifies only when the target tool's capability ceiling is provably inert (`trust::is_inert_for_text_derived_declassification`, an exhaustive 15-kind allowlist) and declared `pure`; otherwise mandatory approval, unconditionally — overriding even a tool's own `Approval<never_require>`. The confused-deputy scenario the red-team found is now a permanent regression test (`tests/test_tool_pipeline.cpp`'s `ADR-023 P2-T2`). 150/150 tests green, zero regressions. **Not shipped** (named residuals, §7): format coverage beyond the 4 built-in rows, the `ae:codec` WASM plugin path, streaming decode. |
| [026](ADR-026-milestone-status-doc-accuracy-and-drift-lint.md) | Are `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md`, and the marketing site's milestone-status claims consistent with the real, landed code — and is any drift between them structurally caught, or only found by accident? | **Judged.** Found (via the same audit, independently re-verified) that all three independently claimed "Milestones 7-9 have not started" or worse; the marketing site's copy additionally folded in the already-complete Milestone 6 and claimed zero implementation exists for MCP/A2A/AG-UI, contradicted by real, tested headers. Corrected all three to a three-state description (complete / in progress, exit criterion not yet met / not started). Shipped `tools/milestone_status_lint.py`, wired into CI with no `continue-on-error`, sentence-scoped (not paragraph-scoped) specifically to avoid a self-collision bug found during design review — the naive version would have falsely re-flagged the corrected Milestone-7 sentence because of the adjacent, unrelated Milestone-8/9 clause. Proven both directions before merge: a positive control (run against the original stale text) actually fails with 5 findings; the corrected text passes clean. Named, not fixed here: `apiContent.ts`'s deeper per-protocol notes and RFC status table carry the same class of staleness at a larger scope than this change verified. |
| [025](ADR-025-naming-lint-namespace-scope-and-027-vocabulary-diagram.md) | Was 027's G1 naming-lint gate actually checking every exported name in `agentengine`'s public core, or only a subset the scanner happened to see? | **Judged, scanner fix only — bulk reconciliation explicitly deferred.** Found (via a full-codebase audit, then independently re-verified) that `tools/naming_lint.py` matched the exact string `"agentengine"` only, silently skipping any C++17 nested-namespace module — 24 headers across `trust/`(7), `sandbox/`(4), `workflow/`(9), and `core/`'s `schema`/`json`/`yaml`/`response_format_codec`(4), ~70+ declarations, entirely unaudited by a real, required CI gate with no `continue-on-error`. Included 027 §4's own flagship worked examples (`Executor`/`Edge`/`Workflow`/`EdgeFailurePolicy` in `graph.hpp`) and the capability-token/bearer-secret public surface (`CapabilityToken`, `SecretKey`, `HmacSha256`). Fixed to recognize `agentengine::<module>` as in-scope except `::detail` (and anything nested under it); wire-protocol namespaces stay excluded via the pre-existing directory-level filter, unaffected. Measured before/after on the real tree: 76 unlisted + 112 suppressed (188) before, 136 unlisted + 127 suppressed (263) after — both a real `exit 1`, the gate still honestly failing, now against the complete surface. 027 §6's namespace diagram corrected to list the real layout. Deliberately does **not** attempt the ~263-name bulk reconciliation against 027 §2-4 in this same change — red-team found that fixing the scanner and reconciling in one pass risked landing a technically-larger-but-still-incomplete table update; sequencing (fix and prove the gate first, triage separately) is recorded as the decision, not the reconciliation itself. |
| [024](ADR-024-skill-scoped-tool-and-mount-wiring.md) | Should a skill's `allowed-tools` additionally gate which tools are declared/invocable (not just which content is readable), and how does a mounted skill's content become reachable by a real sandboxed tool call at all? | **Judged, with a same-day addendum.** Surveyed MAF/Anthropic/OpenAI: none vary tool declaration by loaded-resource state, all scope only content. User directed a deliberate departure anyway — `core/skill_tool_scoping.hpp` computes one `ToolTable` per run, used for BOTH declaration and every `invoke_tool(...)` call (the real enforcement boundary is `invoke_tool`'s own step-1 `table.find()` against the CALLER's table, not `ContextContribution.tools` — filtering only the latter would be cosmetic). A real, independent bug was found and fixed while wiring the other half: `SkillsProvider`'s `Mount.mount_id` was a path-shaped string that could never have matched native_jail's single-path-segment mount-id convention; renamed to the bare skill name. `native_jail::materialize_skill_mounts` (new) bridges `SkillsProvider::mounted()` into real host directories via the previously-uncalled `materialize_mount`. **Addendum (same day):** narrowed further from "every resolved skill, unconditionally" to agent-triggered on demand — a new always-available `mount_skill(name)` tool, backed by `MountedSkillsState` (real, persistent, per-run state, not a chat-history message the way MAF's own stateless/advisory `load_skill` is — verified directly in MAF's C#/Python source that `read_skill_resource`/`run_skill_script` never actually check `load_skill` ran first). Mounting grants no new authority (I3) — every resolved skill's files are already readable unconditionally; mounting only activates already-pre-authorized tool declarations. Negative-control-proven offline (a real tool rejected by `invoke_tool` when unmounted, succeeding after) and run live end to end against a real OpenRouter model twice — once reading a mounted skill's content via real mediated `open()`, once self-sequencing `mount_skill` before `execute_code` purely because the tool wasn't declared yet. Residuals: no type-level guarantee ties the declared/invocable tables together (convention only); only `native_jail`'s Python runner is wired, not `MediatedShellRunner`/`wasm`; `AgentSession` still owns no sandbox/tool-call loop in production, so `MountedSkillsState` sharing is a process-scoped-static workaround, not a general mechanism. |
| [028](ADR-028-session-scoped-stateful-tools.md) | Does closing "`Tool<>::invoke()` has no path to `AgentSession`-owned state" (ADR-024 §8's named residual) require a new type-erasure mechanism, or can it stay inside this codebase's existing conventions? | **Judged.** First draft (extend `ContextProvider` with an optional `StateT`-aware `on_context` overload) rejected by red-team on the strongest grounds: it solves a problem that doesn't need solving — `HistoryProviderT history_provider_` is already a per-`AgentSession`-instance member, so a state-aware provider can already own its own session-scoped state as ordinary members, exactly the pattern `SkillsProvider`/`HistoryAndSkillsProvider` already establish; zero core-seam change needed. Confirmed exhaustively this session: this codebase has never used `std::any`/`std::type_index`/`typeid`/`dynamic_cast` anywhere (RTTI avoided on principle, both AgentEngine and vendored Quark) — ruling out a type-erased `void*` slot as foreign to convention. Accepted design: `make_tool_descriptor_with_invoke<ToolT>(InvokeFn)` (`tool_pipeline.hpp`), preserving `ToolT`'s compile-time-checked `Capabilities<...>`/`Approval<...>`/`EffectClass<...>` extraction while letting a provider's own member function supply the actual invoke logic. Red-team found one **fatal** hazard, fixed structurally not by convention: a state-capturing closure marked `Backgroundable` is a real dangling-reference/data-race hazard (`start_background_task()` already detaches a real thread with no synchronization against `fork_from()`/`clear_in_process_state()`, neither of which is `protocol`-serialized) — closed via a new `ToolDescriptor::captures_session_state` flag checked and refused in `background_task()`'s own authorize step, before step 8. Proven: state persists correctly across rounds within one run; two independent sessions never see each other's state (would produce a wrong, shared total if they did); the Backgroundable-conflict combination is refused with a specific error code, no thread ever detached; the existing `make_tool_descriptor<ToolT>()` path is completely unaffected. 179/180 tests pass (same pre-existing, unrelated failure ADR-024/027 already recorded — corrected 2026-08-11: two real, deterministic test-authoring bugs, not a flake; see ADR-024 §6). Explicitly not claimed: real CodeAct wiring (`MediatedPythonRunner`/`MountedSkillsState`) is separate, later work; the interpreter's own one-per-process constraint (a scoped ADR-002 security-mechanism decision, not a CPython law — subinterpreters are a real, still-open candidate per ADR-002 §6 item 5) is untouched; "provider-owned state must never hold a capability" is a real, type-system-unenforceable invariant, named with the same prominence ADR-024 gives its own unenforced invariants. **Same-day addendum:** `fork_from()`/`clear_in_process_state()` now copy/reset `history_provider_` too (a real gap this ADR's own §6 first only named — re-examining the prior code showed the old "leave it untouched" choice was correct before this ADR, wrong after; a fork's counter now correctly continues from the source's copied value, and a deleted session's provider state leaves no residue), proven in the same test file's S5. |
| [027](ADR-027-agent-session-tool-call-loop.md) | Should `AgentSession::handle()`'s tool-call loop live inside the actor's own coroutine (one `StartRun` → N internal rounds → one final response), or stay external, formalized into a reusable helper instead of three drifting hand-rolled copies? | **Judged.** User directed "inside," matching `turn_index`'s own pre-existing intended-shape comment. Red-team found one **fatal** issue in the first draft: `ToolCall::provenance` wasn't threaded into `ToolCallRequest::provenance`, silently defaulting every call to `vendor_structured` and reopening the exact confused-deputy gap ADR-023 §4b Finding 1 closed (a bug that already existed, independently, in all three external-loop copies this ADR replaces — never previously exercised unattended). Fixed once, in a new shared `tool_call_extraction.hpp`, and regression-verified both directions during design (the reintroduced bug was confirmed to make the new test's provenance assertion fail; the fix was confirmed to make it pass again). Also confirmed (not merely assumed) that the two live e2e tests would break and `cli_chat.cpp`'s external loop would go dead the moment this shipped — all three rewritten in the same change. `ToolTable::from_descriptors(contribution->tools)` rebuilt fresh every round from the same snapshot used to declare tools that round closes, for this loop, the "declared ≠ invocable" gap ADR-024 §3a/§7 named as convention-only. 178/179 tests pass (the one failure, `test_mediated_python_runner_hostile_corpus`, is the same pre-existing, unrelated failure ADR-024 §6 already recorded — corrected 2026-08-11: two real, deterministic test-authoring bugs, not a flake). Residuals named, not fixed: synchronous tool calls block the Quark worker thread (native tools must stay non-blocking); approval-gated tools with no configured decider are indistinguishable from ordinary non-convergence; null `capabilities_` denies per-call rather than aborting the run; `on_turn_end`'s per-round `TurnView` no longer includes the run's input message; `AgentMetadata::max_turns` still isn't wired to the new runtime `max_turns_`; no mid-loop checkpointing. Session-scoped stateful tools (CodeAct/skills) and suspend-for-human approval are explicitly out of scope, decided before design started, not discovered gaps. |
| [031](ADR-031-spawn-cost-budget-actor-primitive.md) | Does routing a consumable spawn-token pool's check-and-decrement through a real Quark actor's own `Sequential` dispatch actually close the concurrent double-spend 026 §9 Q1's own already-red-teamed sketch predicts a bare copyable value type (like `SpawnBudget`'s depth-ceiling shape) would suffer? | **Judged.** Not a fresh design — 026 §9 Q1 already contains a full design → red-team cycle (2026-08-11) explicitly marked "no code, not an ADR"; this ADR turns that sketch into real, tested code. `SpawnCostBudgetActor` (`trust/spawn_cost_budget.hpp`): a real `quark::Actor<..., quark::Sequential>` holding one consumable `std::uint64_t` pool, deliberately a SEPARATE type from `SpawnBudget` (depth is a ceiling, safely re-derivable by concurrent siblings from the same parent value; tokens are a consumed pool, where two concurrent spawns each attenuating a bare copy could each succeed past the true remaining amount). Check-and-decrement happens as one step inside the actor's own Sequential handler, closing the race by construction via Quark's own already-relied-upon dispatch guarantee, no hand-written lock. One deliberate deviation from `AgentSession`'s own "never respond on failure" idiom, explained not silently copied: that idiom is safe only under `TestKit`'s synchronous resolution — `reply_cell.hpp`'s own comment confirms an unanswered `Ask` under a REAL engine only resolves at actor teardown, which would hang a live caller — so this actor always responds, using `result<SpawnTokenGrant>`. Proven under a REAL, multi-worker `quark::Engine` (not `TestKit`, which cannot produce a genuine race): 8 concurrent `std::thread` callers contending 130 tokens each against a 1000-token pool (guaranteed contention, not a lucky fit) never grant more than 1000 total across 5 consecutive re-runs, with the actor's final `remaining()` exactly reconciling against the sum granted every time. Not claimed: `agent.spawn` itself still has zero real call path anywhere in this codebase (re-confirmed by the same exhaustive survey ADR-030 independently ran) — no nested-agent-run invocation mechanism, no sub-worktree wiring, no production `AgentSession` tool-call loop to host a spawned child; this ADR proves the standalone cost-pool primitive only, matching ADR-006's own precedent for the depth half. No refill/top-up mechanism (deliberate); no attribution/audit trail; wall-clock/deadline budgeting stays fully open. |
| [032](ADR-032-workflow-executor-worktree-scoping.md) | Can a per-workflow-executor `sharing_mode` policy be decided and turned into a real, capability-gated worktree grant using ONLY 025 §3's already-shipped `SubWorktree`/`Mount` primitives, closing 014 §1's named "nothing states whether a workflow executor gets its own sub-worktree, inherits the caller's, or shares one" gap? | **Judged.** Independent red-team (via the `Agent` tool) on the FIRST draft found one fatal flaw (a `readonly` grant cannot actually be mounted — `Mount`/`mount_read` has no pinned-digest read path, only a `Ref`-backed one) and six must-fix issues (resume would silently re-branch and clobber in-progress work; the suggested `run_id()`-derived precondition is temporally impossible; unsanitized executor ids splice into worktree/mount paths; `WorkflowBuilder`'s C++ authoring surface had no way to actually opt an executor into `shared`; `branch`'s whole-parent-tree initial content was undocumented and untested; `graph.hpp` would have swallowed `worktree.hpp`'s full actor/persistence/capability dependency graph for one enum) — all seven fixed before the accepted design shipped, not dropped as residuals. Accepted design: `Executor::worktree_mode` (`workflow/graph.hpp`) defaults every executor to `branch` UNCONDITIONALLY (not 025 §3's "sequential defaults to shared" applied per-node — `WorkflowSupervisor`'s superstep model means any two executors reachable in the same round get concurrent asks, and general graphs here have cycles and dynamic routing that make proving two nodes structurally can't co-occur unsound to attempt), with `shared`/`readonly`/`scratch` reachable as an explicit per-executor opt-in via `TypedExecutor`'s own field. `workflow/worktree_scoping.hpp`'s `mint_executor_worktrees`/`resume_executor_worktrees` turn the declaration into a real `SubWorktree` + capability-gated `Mount`, fail closed on a double-mint (`worktree_scoping.already_minted`) rather than silently re-branching over existing work, and fail closed rather than fabricate on an unreconstructible resume (`readonly`'s pinned digest lives nowhere durable — `worktree_scoping.readonly_resume_unsupported`). Proven directly against `mount_read`/`mount_write` (not through a live `FunctionExecutor` — no production host builds one yet), 11 test blocks / 47 checks, each tracing to a specific red-team finding rather than a generic happy-path sweep, including a simulated-restart resume proof (M8) and a pre-existing-content-visible-through-a-fresh-branch proof (M5) closing the "proof plan would pass regardless" complaint the red-team raised about the first draft. Not claimed: no wiring into a running `FunctionExecutor`'s `EffectContext`; `agent`/`sub_workflow`-kind executors untouched (not built); merge-on-join unbuilt; `readonly` executors have no guest-facing `Mount` at all (needs a `core/worktree.hpp` primitive change, out of scope). |
| [033](ADR-033-middleware-model-call-chain.md) | Can 002 §5's model-call `Middleware<Ms...>` interception point be turned into real, composed code using the same decorator-wrapper shape `ResilientChatClient<Inner>` already established, while genuinely satisfying "may not widen capabilities"? | **Judged.** Independent red-team found ONE FATAL, exploitable flaw: a `before_model`/`after_model` hook can freely rewrite `ChatResponse.message.content`, and a `ToolCall` defaults to the FULLY TRUSTED `call_provenance::vendor_structured` — `invoke_tool`'s step 5 checks that only against the target tool's own `approval_mode` (may be `never_require`). A middleware fabricating or mutating a `ToolCall` therefore launders an untrusted decision through the exact channel a genuine vendor-returned call travels — the confused-deputy shape ADR-023 already closed once for raw model text, relocated to a middleware content rewrite. Closed by `enforce_backend_tool_call_provenance`: any `ToolCall` in the final response not byte-identical to one the REAL backend actually returned is forced to `call_provenance::text_derived` before the wrapper ever returns it, routing it through ADR-023's stricter gate (which overrides even `never_require` for anything with a real capability ceiling or non-pure effect class) — proven both for fabrication (a genuinely clean backend response gets an appended forged call) and for mutation of an already-genuine call's arguments, with a mandatory positive control proving an untouched genuine call keeps its trust (the mechanism is targeted, not a blanket downgrade). Three further must-fix gaps closed: §5's own "attributed to it by name in the trace" constraint had no wiring at all (fixed: every hook-bearing middleware must declare a compile-time `name`, an optional caller-injected trace hook fires structured attribution events, mirroring `WorkflowSupervisor::CheckpointHook`'s established idiom); composition order with `ResilientChatClient` was unspecified despite the two orders NOT being interchangeable (documented as a mandatory contract — `MiddlewareChatClient` must be the OUTER layer, never the inner, or retries would re-run the whole before-phase under one "stable" idempotency key and a fallback-content hook could silently corrupt circuit-breaker health tracking); hook-exception semantics were undefined (fixed: a throwing hook is caught and converted to a `result<T>` failure, never escapes as a raw exception, matching every other control-flow surface in this codebase). Also independently confirmed and fixed: 002 §5's own snippet wrote the hook return type as `ae::task<>`, which `quark/core/task.hpp` confirms has no co_await protocol at all and is unbuildable as written — corrected to `ae::task<std::monostate>` (RFC text updated to match, per CLAUDE.md's "fix the spec first" rule). `MiddlewareChatClient<Inner, Ms...>` (`core/middleware.hpp`) is the accepted mechanism: a `ChatClient`-conforming template decorator (same shape as `ResilientChatClient<Inner>`, never a virtual base), first-registered-outermost ordering, forward before-phase/backward after-phase onion unwind, `ModelCallContext` deliberately carrying no `EffectContext&`/capability type at all (I2 enforced by omission, not a runtime check). 15 test blocks / 30 checks, all CORRECT. Scoped to the model-call interception point only — run/turn/tool-call points and `AgentSession` wiring are explicitly not attempted, matching ADR-028's own "prove the mechanism against one real consumer" precedent; streaming (`chat_stream`) is forwarded uninterceped. **Amendment (2026-08-12):** `MiddlewareChatClient` itself was REMOVED the same day as `FailoverChatClient`/`ResilientChatClient` (this repo had shipped nowhere, so no deprecation-then-migration cost) — `MiddlewareModelCallGateway<Inner, Ms...>` (ADR-036) is the real consumer of this ADR's own machinery (`ModelCallContext`/`run_before`/`run_after`/`enforce_backend_tool_call_provenance`, all reused verbatim) now; its regression suite (T1-T15) survives, ported to the new wrapper in `tests/test_middleware_model_call_gateway.cpp`. |
| [030](ADR-030-session-scoped-codeact-wiring.md) | Can ADR-028's `make_tool_descriptor_with_invoke` mechanism give CodeAct real per-session state, and which of `cli_chat.cpp`'s five process-global statics (`MediatedPythonRunner`, `ExecState`, `MountedSkillsState`, mount roots, a duplicate `SkillsProvider<>`) can actually become per-session vs. which must stay process-wide? | **Judged (2026-08-13).** First draft (each session's provider owns its own `MediatedPythonRunner`) rejected by red-team on two fatal, independently-confirmed grounds: `MediatedPythonRunner::run()`/`refresh_agent_tools()` route through unsynchronized, process-wide globals (`g_current_ctx`, `config_.tool_bridge`), so two sessions each owning a runner and calling it concurrently is a real cross-session confused-deputy leak, not just a crash; and cwd/env/the work-mount directory are OS-process-global, so a genuinely per-session `ExecState` can't isolate two sessions through one interpreter regardless of ownership (CPython's classic embedding API supports exactly one `Py_InitializeFromConfig` per process). Accepted: the runner stays a process-wide singleton, reached through a new `CodeActRunnerBinding<RunnerT>` that makes ADR-002 §5.5.6's "one interpreter per process" rule structurally unbreakable (first session to `bind()` claims it for the process's life; any other session_id fails closed) rather than merely commented — closing the confused-deputy hazard by making it impossible for a second session to ever reach the runner at all. What DOES become genuinely per-session: `MountedSkillsState`/`ExecState`, now real `ToolDeclaringHistoryProvider` members reached via `make_tool_descriptor_with_invoke`, safe specifically because the claim guarantees at most one session is ever live against the shared runner. A new `AgentSession::history_provider()` accessor lets `main()` configure the provider post-construction (the seam ADR-028 §6 left unbuilt). Proven: the claim primitive itself (first-caller-wins, idempotent same-session re-bind, fails closed for a different one) and, at full-stack integration (two real `AgentSession`s, a real tool-call round loop), that `MountedSkillsState` genuinely never leaks between sessions and the claim is enforced at the point of USE, not just at configure time (an unconfigured/rejected session is denied on every attempt to reach the shared runner). `tools/cli_chat.cpp` itself rebuilds and links clean against the corrected design. Not claimed: true multi-tenant CodeAct (two sessions BOTH executing Python) is still impossible — this ADR makes the second session's rejection safe, not the scenario possible; `fork_from()`'s interaction with a bound runner claim is a named, unexercised residual (a forked session inherits a pointer to a binding still bound to the source's id, and nothing re-validates it against the fork's own identity — `cli_chat.cpp` never forks, so unexercised in production); the shared "work" mount directory isn't forked either, matching ADR-028's own precedent for that class of gap. |
| [029](ADR-029-suspend-for-human-approval.md) | Can a round genuinely suspend mid-tool-loop for a real human's approval answer — resumable from a separate ask, continuing the same run/turn — without overflowing Quark's fixed message-pool cap or duplicating the whole round-loop body for the resume path? | **Judged (2026-08-13).** First draft (widen `StartRun` with an optional resuming-interaction-id field) rejected by red-team on a real compiled measurement: `sizeof(quark::Ask<StartRun, AgentResponse>)` would grow from 160 to 200 bytes, over Quark's hard 192-byte `MessagePool::kMaxPayload`. Accepted: a separate `ResolveInteraction` message (measured 136 bytes) plus factoring the round loop into a shared, genuinely-awaitable `run_rounds<AskT>()` (`quark::task<std::monostate>`, per ADR-047 — a bare `task<>` cannot be `co_await`ed by a sibling handler) that both `StartRun` and `ResolveInteraction` drive. Red-team found and closed six issues before acceptance, the sharpest being: validate a resume against `history_`'s actual pending-call tail BEFORE resolving the `Interaction` (not after, which could resolve against a round nobody actually reviewed); reject a fresh `StartRun` while a round is suspended (would otherwise interleave a second concurrent run); admission-check `ResolveInteraction::caller` the same way `StartRun::caller` already is (an unrelated principal must not resolve someone else's pending approval by guessing an id); a resume continues the SAME `run_id`/`turn_index`, never a fresh run (I4/I8). Proven in `tests/test_agent_session_suspend_approval.cpp` (27/27 assertions, deterministic/offline): suspension fires before any call invokes; a second `StartRun` while suspended never resolves; `approved=true` invokes the exact pending call for real and converges; `approved=false` feeds back a denial without ever invoking the tool; an unknown interaction id fails closed touching nothing; a mismatched caller is denied at admission before the lookup runs. Opt-in only (`suspend_for_approval_`, default false) — with it off, or with a real `ApprovalDecider` configured, behavior is unchanged from ADR-027. Not claimed: `Interaction::expires_at_ns` remains unwired (a suspended run with no human answer waits forever); the suspended round's own pending message isn't durably checkpointed yet (the same `Message`/`ContentItem` serialization gap ADR-027/028 already name); no partial (per-call, sub-round) approval; no real approval UI/transport — session-actor mechanism only. |
| [034](ADR-034-agentsession-streaming-turn-loop.md) | Can `AgentSession::handle()`'s internal loop stream each round's model call for real, live token-by-token rendering in `cli_chat.cpp`, without breaking failover/circuit-breaker-feedback/response-format-leak-scanning for every OTHER caller, and without letting 004 §5's `TokenBudget<N>` silently stop being enforced? | **Judged.** First draft (`chat_stream()` as `handle()`'s ONLY model-call path, unconditional) rejected by red-team on three independent fatal grounds, each read directly from the real backend source: `FailoverChatClient::chat_stream()` never falls over to a secondary backend (a named, deliberate scoping decision in that file); `ResilientChatClient::chat_stream()` never reports an outcome to its own circuit breaker; and `OpenAIChatClient`'s ADR-023 confused-deputy leak-scan (`apply_response_format_scan`) runs only from `chat()`, never `chat_stream()` — an unconditional streaming core loop would silently drop all three for every caller composing them under `AgentSession<ChatClientT, ...>`, not just the CLI. Accepted: streaming is a per-session opt-in (`set_stream_model_calls(bool)`, default false, same shape as `suspend_for_approval_`) — every existing/default caller keeps calling `chat()` bit-for-bit (re-proven by re-running `test_agent_session_tool_call_loop.cpp`'s full R1-R6 unmodified); an opted-in session accepts losing the three protections above for itself, made visible (not silent) via a `run_event_kind::warning` fired once per streaming run. A second, independently real gap closed: `ChatResponseUpdate` had no usage field at all, so streaming with no safeguard would let `TokenBudget<N>` silently stop being enforced — fixed by adding `std::optional<Usage> usage` to `ChatResponseUpdate` (additive), wiring `OpenAIChatClient`'s streaming path to request/parse OpenRouter's trailing `stream_options.include_usage` chunk, and making `AgentSession`'s new `run_model_call()` helper fail the run CLOSED (`run.usage_unavailable`, ask never resolves) whenever a stream completes with no usage — never silently zero-cost. Message reconstruction from accumulated deltas needs no merge step: `tool_call_extraction.hpp`'s `tool_calls_of()`/`text_of()` are genuinely position/count-agnostic across however many content items a Message carries (verified, not assumed). Proven in `tests/test_agent_session_streaming_model_calls.cpp` (S1-S4, 16 checks, a real `chat_stream()`-backed scripted ChatClientT built on `core/stream.hpp`'s real `make_stream<T>`): streamed no-tool-call responses reconstruct correctly with real `model_delta` events firing per delta; a streamed multi-round tool-call conversation converges correctly through the unmodified invocation pipeline; a stream with no terminal usage fails closed; the accessor pair defaults false and round-trips. `tools/cli_chat.cpp` migrated off `quark::TestKit` (documented as fully synchronous, "no threads, no wall-clock" — genuinely incapable of hosting concurrent live printing) onto a real single-worker `quark::Engine`, the same real-Engine-hosting-an-AgentSession shape `test_agent_session_suspend_resume.cpp` already established; a per-turn `std::jthread` drain thread prints `model_delta` text as it arrives with RAII-guaranteed `request_stop()`+`join()` on every exit path. Verified live against a real OpenRouter session (`api-test.txt`): streamed turns completed with real usage captured (no `run.usage_unavailable`), tool pipeline and skill mounting unaffected. Not claimed: `AnthropicChatClient`'s streaming path is not wired for usage capture this pass (fails closed if used, does not silently miscount); the leak-scan gap is named, not closed, for the streaming path itself (only made visible via the warning event); `cli_chat.cpp`'s own Engine-hosting shape has no dedicated regression test of its own, resting on the cited precedent plus the live run. Full suite: 206/206 pass. **Amendment (2026-08-12):** `FailoverChatClient`/`ResilientChatClient`, cited above for their `chat_stream()` scoping limitations, were REMOVED that day — ADR-036's `ModelCallGateway` is the real, streaming-capable successor with retry/failover/breaker feedback all genuinely working, closing the gap this red-team finding only worked around. |
| [035](ADR-035-chatclient-streaming-completeness.md) | Can every `ChatClient` conformer become genuinely streaming-capable — closing ADR-034's two named residuals (Anthropic usage, the leak-scan gap) and converting every dead `chat_stream()` stub to real streaming — ending with `chat()` no longer required by the concept at all, without silently weakening any protection or losing coverage? | **Judged.** Four phases, each independently built/tested/committed. Phase 1a: `AnthropicChatClient`'s `StreamingUpdateAccumulator` now wires its own already-tested `message_start`/`message_delta` usage-reduce functions to actually populate `ChatResponseUpdate::usage` (previously computed but never attached) — closing the gap that would otherwise fail every Anthropic streaming call closed once `chat()` is gone. Phase 1b: the ADR-023 leak-scan, previously reachable only through `OpenAIChatClient::chat()`, relocated to a shared `core/response_format_leak_scan.hpp` and applied uniformly by `AgentSession::run_model_call()` regardless of backend or streaming — an independent red-team pass found a real double-scan laundering hazard in the naive version (a second pass could re-decode a first pass's own diagnostic text and promote a different, attacker-chosen candidate) closed by skipping already-`tainted` items, proven via a dedicated regression test (L4) rather than mere absence-of-failure. Phase 2 (giving the three `chat()`-only wrapper templates real `chat_stream()` parity) was attempted and abandoned after red-team found a genuine, unresolved use-after-free hazard specific to middleware hooks under a non-coroutine `chat_stream()` — see ADR-036, this ADR's own superseding replacement for that piece. Phase 3: a full-codebase survey found 46 files with dead, type-satisfying-only `chat_stream()` stubs (never exercised by any test) plus two production call sites (`MemoryProvider`/`HistoryProvider<Summarize<...>>`) still calling `chat()` directly; all 46 converted mechanically (five parallel `Agent` batches, same prescriptive pattern, zero test assertions touched, 208/208), both production call sites moved to a new shared `core/chat_stream_drain.hpp` helper (also backfilled into `ModelCallGateway`'s own duplicate copy, a pure refactor reverified against its own suite), and only then — with every conformer independently proven capable — `chat()` dropped from `ChatClient`'s `requires` clause (widening what can satisfy the concept, deleting nothing, 208/208 unchanged before and after). Not claimed: `chat()` itself still exists on every real conformer and on the three legacy wrapper templates (unmodified, their formal fate a separate future decision); `quark::error`'s coarser failure vocabulary remains a real, named limitation versus `chat()`'s own richer classification; no new live-network run was added beyond what ADR-034 already established. **Amendment (2026-08-12):** the "separate future decision" on the three legacy wrapper templates was made the same day — `FailoverChatClient`/`ResilientChatClient`/`MiddlewareChatClient` REMOVED (unshipped anywhere, no deprecation cost), superseded by ADR-036's `ModelCallGateway`/`MiddlewareModelCallGateway`. A code-review pass on this ADR's own diff also found and fixed two real gaps: `chat_stream_drain.hpp`'s `drained_failure_to_agent_error()` hardcoded `failure_class::transient` regardless of the underlying error (real I2 hazard — a permanent denial could be retried forever by a `failure_class`-keyed consumer), and `AgentSession::run_model_call()`'s non-gateway branch still unconditionally required `chat()` to compile, making the concept relaxation fictional for its one real consumer — both fixed, see the ADR document's own §7a. |
| [036](ADR-036-model-call-gateway.md) | Once the naive plan to give `FailoverChatClient`/`ResilientChatClient`/`MiddlewareChatClient` real `chat_stream()` parity hit a confirmed, unresolved use-after-free hazard (a middleware hook coroutine driven through a non-coroutine `chat_stream()` via a "resume once and hope" trick can be resumed later by whatever it awaited, touching long-gone stack state), what actually delivers retry/failover/middleware under streaming safely? | **Judged.** Two small, separately-testable types instead of one that owns everything (a C++ class template can have only one trailing parameter pack, so `Fallback...` and `Ms...` can't share a template anyway, and the project owner separately directed against folding this into `AgentSession`, which already owns the turn loop/tools/approval/budgets/history/leak-scan): `ModelCallGateway<Primary, Fallback...>` (retry + one real `quark::CircuitBreaker` per backend + failover, reusing `RetryPolicy`/`BreakerConfig` verbatim) and `MiddlewareModelCallGateway<Inner, Ms...>` (middleware hooks over any `ModelCallGatewayLike` `Inner`, reusing ADR-033's `ModelCallContext`/`run_before`/`run_after`/`enforce_backend_tool_call_provenance` verbatim). Both expose exactly one method, `call(request, ctx) -> task<result<ChatResponse>>` — a REAL coroutine, so a middleware hook's `co_await` is completely ordinary, structurally closing the use-after-free hazard rather than defending against it at runtime. `AgentSession<ChatClientT, ...>`'s own template signature is unchanged (62 existing call sites unaffected) — its `requires` clause widened to accept either `ChatClient` or the new `ModelCallGatewayLike` concept, dispatched via `if constexpr` in `run_model_call()`; a gateway-routed round trades live `model_delta` events for the reliability guarantee, named via a one-time warning event matching ADR-034's own pattern. A second, independent red-team pass on the drafted design (before implementation) found and closed three further concrete issues: the enclosing class's own `requires ChatClient<ChatClientT>` clause needed to change too, or the new branch would be dead code; a naively default-constructed `std::array<CircuitBreaker,N>` would silently ship non-functional (zero-cooldown) breakers; and the required `OpenAIChatClient::chat_stream()` HTTP-status co-fix (needed so retry decisions are meaningful against the real backend, not just test fixtures) must classify by status code only, never reuse the non-streaming path's dynamic body-derived message, since `quark::error::detail` is a non-owning `string_view` fed from a detached background thread's soon-dead stack. Proven: `tests/test_model_call_gateway.cpp`, 20 checks — retry, non-retryable-fails-fast-then-fails-over, a tripped breaker sheds admission with an exact call-count proof (not timing-inferred), the middleware fatal-finding fix (fabricated `ToolCall` forced to `text_derived`) reachable through the new composition path, and full `AgentSession` integration (zero live deltas, the warning fires). Full suite 208/208, both before and after a later refactor shared the gateway's internal drain loop with ADR-035's memory/history provider work. Not claimed: the three original wrapper templates are unmodified, not deprecated, not migrated off; `quark::errc`'s retry classification stays structurally coarser than `chat()`'s own; no live-network verification specific to this ADR (rests on the real backends' own separately-verified `chat_stream()`); the last-attempt-could-stream-live optimization was considered and deliberately not built. **Amendment (2026-08-12):** the three original wrapper templates (`FailoverChatClient`/`ResilientChatClient`/`MiddlewareChatClient`) were REMOVED the same day — unshipped anywhere, so no deprecation-then-migration cost; `RetryPolicy`/`BreakerConfig`/`real_jitter` (still shared with this ADR's own types) moved from the now-deleted `resilient_chat_client.hpp` to a new `retry_policy.hpp`. A code-review pass also named (not fixed) a real residual: `attempt_with_retry`'s backoff blocks the calling actor-worker thread (`sleep_for`, no `co_await` across the retry loop) — pre-existing, `ResilientChatClient::chat()` had the identical pattern; a real fix needs a coroutine-suspending timer primitive that does not exist anywhere in Quark's `task<>` today. |
| [038](ADR-038-rt-project-passivation-semantics.md) | Once Quark's actor engine (and its `.passivate()`/lazy-reactivation mechanism) is removed, is there anything real left behind 030 §4's "Pause" verb, or does it reduce to a bare status flip? | **Judged.** What survives: passivation always did two things — evict from memory, and flush durable state first. Removing the actor engine removes the former completely (a plain `rt::AgentSession` has no runtime managing its residency); the latter survives as `rt::ProjectSupervisor::checkpoint_members_and_workflows()`, and `rt::pause_project()`/`archive_project()` flip the manifest's status ONLY if every checkpoint succeeds (a real, deliberate fail-closed choice the Quark original's bare-`bool` `passivate()` had no shape to make). 030 §7 G1's own scale-isolation gate is reproven (`test_rt_project_scale_isolation.cpp`, N=100) but its latency-regression half is deliberately NOT reproven — `rt::` land has no shared scheduler/lock left for `pause_project` to accidentally contend on, so a timing check here could never fail; the structural isolation check (pausing #50 touches only store #50) stands in for it, proving the hazard class can't arise rather than "wasn't observed." Not claimed: no eviction/reactivation exists to reprove (the Quark original's own Dormant-census claim has no `rt::` equivalent, named not dropped); no sandbox-count claim (matching the Quark original's own scope limit); no retention/GC for `archive_project()`. `project/lifecycle.hpp`/`project/project.hpp` (the old Quark originals) deleted outright, zero remaining real consumers once their two test files were superseded. |
| [039](ADR-039-inbound-transport-host-pluggable.md) | Should AgentEngine build a first-party TLS+HTTP listener for MCP/A2A (ADR-021/022's direction), or expose only transport-agnostic protocol handlers and let the embedding application (or a reverse proxy) supply the actual socket/TLS layer, matching MAF's `MapMcp()`-on-Kestrel and the OpenAI Agents SDK's uvicorn/Starlette model? | **Judged.** Supersedes ADR-021 §3-8 and ADR-022 in full. Reopened because ADR-037 made ADR-022's "which reactor" question moot (its Quark types are gone) and because this project's own dispatchers (`McpServer::dispatch`, `A2aServer`, AG-UI's `to_sse_frame`) were already transport-agnostic in-process functions — ADR-021 recorded that fact but never followed it to "so let the host supply the transport," building a first-party listener anyway. Accepted: no first-party listener, ever, for v1 — a host-pluggable `InboundTransportRequest`/`InboundTransportResponse` boundary is the target shape (not yet plumbed into the real dispatchers — real follow-on work, not claimed done). Independent red-team found two FATAL issues in the first draft: (1) the draft assumed a per-call `Principal` that doesn't exist on the real `dispatch()`/`send_message()` signatures; (2) the naive fix — construct a fresh dispatcher per HTTP request — collides with `McpServer`'s own documented "must outlive every task it starts" contract (a backgrounded `tasks/call` completion firing into freed memory), a lifetime hazard structurally identical to ADR-021's own request-smuggling-as-forgery finding, just relocated from a pooled connection to a short-lived object. Fixed by design correction, not by claiming the plumbing exists: one dispatcher instance per AUTHENTICATED SESSION (never per-request), held via shared ownership for at least as long as its connection lives, as a binding contract on every host-pluggable transport adapter. A third MUST-FIX (no bridge from verified bearer-token claims to a `Principal`, so every adapter would hand-roll its own inconsistently) was closed with real code this pass: `trust::principal_from_bearer_claims()` (`trust/bearer_token.hpp`), proven in `tests/test_principal_from_bearer_claims.cpp` (9/9 checks) — sub/tenant_id map correctly, `kind` is an explicit non-token-derived override, no other claim leaks through, distinct subjects never collide. Two residuals named, not closed: `A2aServer` has zero principal/tenant scoping today (`a2a/server.hpp`'s own comment already says so), a real gap the moment more than one `Principal` can reach one instance; and a reference/example transport adapter under `examples/` (not core) risks the same "example silently becomes de facto production" drift this project already found once (ADR-026) unless M7's conformance suites are built to run against any conforming adapter, not a hardcoded one — a design constraint named for the harness, not yet built. ADR-021's own real, Judged bearer-token/HMAC mechanism is NOT superseded — it survives unchanged as a reusable primitive a host adapter may call. |
| [037](ADR-037-remove-quark-as-core-runtime.md) | Should AgentEngine stop building its session/workflow/tool runtime on Quark's actor engine and become a self-contained C++ SDK (MAF/OpenAI SDK/Anthropic-shaped), with Quark reduced to an optional deployment target rather than the foundation? | **Judged (2026-08-14).** Phases 1-5 (§7) were EXECUTED (2026-08-13): every `quark::` production dependency was ported to `agentengine::rt::`/vendored standalone and the `third_party/quark` submodule itself was removed from the tree, re-verified via a from-scratch build. The one residual its own completion note flagged at the time (a stale vendored CA-bundle hash pin) was resolved the same day, commit `3962fa2`, 174/174 green — no longer open as of sign-off. Two independent audits (`Explore` agents reading real source, not memory) grounded the question before any design was proposed: distribution/cluster machinery has ZERO footprint in AgentEngine's current build (no includes, no CMake flags, no compiled object code — Quark's own `CMakeLists.txt` compiles only `src/version.cpp`; the cluster/net headers are unused template surface) and is explicitly named in AgentEngine's own RFCs as deferred future work, not accidental baggage — so the stated objection ("distributed components clutter the implementation") does not describe anything currently in the build. What IS deep: `AgentSession`/`WorkflowSupervisor`/`FunctionExecutor` are literal `quark::Actor` CRTP instances (not callers of Quark), the 192-byte mailbox cap shapes message design, `core/stream.hpp`'s own backpressure scheme is shaped around a Quark ring-buffer limitation, persistence goes through `quark::EventLog`/`FenceToken`, and ~42/208 test files drive `quark::TestKit`/`quark::Engine` directly. Proposed target architecture (§4): AgentEngine owns a small `ae::task<T>` coroutine type (no longer a `quark::task<T>` alias); `AgentSession` becomes a plain class with I1 ("one session, one executor") re-derived via an internal in-flight guard instead of actor-mailbox exclusivity; a small, purpose-built thread-pool executor drives I/O continuations (no mailbox, no supervision DSL, no distributed anything); `stream<T>`'s public shape is unchanged, only its backend swaps; persistence becomes a host-injected `SessionStore` interface rather than a runtime-owned mechanism; `WorkflowSupervisor`/`FunctionExecutor` get a hand-rolled supervised-retry loop; `CircuitBreaker` gets reimplemented (small, already self-contained); the test harness gets a small AgentEngine-owned synchronous driver. Red-team findings (self-authored; independent pass recommended before Phase 1): I1 moves from structurally-enforced to runtime-checked, a real regression in safety-by-construction that needs its own enforcement discipline; this invalidates the PROOF ARTIFACTS behind 15+ Judged ADRs (027-036) even though their BEHAVIORAL claims should survive — re-authoring ~42+ test files against the new substrate is likely the single largest cost in this whole effort; the new executor is unproven infrastructure exactly where Quark currently provides a mature, independently-red-teamed scheduler for free — the single largest NEW risk this ADR introduces; persistence's hardest problem (safe concurrent snapshot) is being reimplemented, not relocated, and needs its own design pass. Proposed phasing (§7): Phase 1 builds the new substrate standalone (Quark still runs everything real); Phase 2 migrates `AgentSession`, re-proving each existing ADR's behavioral claim one at a time; Phase 3 migrates workflow; Phase 4 removes the Quark submodule; Phase 5 re-verifies the full suite and retires `AgentEngineSpecification.md`'s "Quark mapping" section. Not claimed: no code exists; the threading-model default (synchronous vs. thread-pool-async) is flagged, not decided; no timeline commitment; Quark's other locked decisions (WASM ABI, embedded CPython, no microvm, CRTP + declarative authoring) are orthogonal and assumed unaffected. **Addendum (2026-08-19):** the executed Phase 1 red-team's own predicted risk ("the new executor is unproven infrastructure... the single largest NEW risk this ADR introduces") materialized for real, found via red-team against decisions/ADR-064-recall-tool-sync-invoke-vs-async-embedder.md's design review: `rt::ThreadPool::run_job()`'s original `while (!done()) resume();` loop was genuine UB (a cross-thread coroutine double-resume) the moment a `ThreadPool`-driven job's `AsyncMutex`/`channel<T>` await genuinely contended — never actually triggered in this codebase (every real job today, including `AgentSession` via `tools/cli_chat.cpp`, happens to complete in exactly one `resume()`), but nothing prevented it. Fixed same day: `run_job()` now resumes exactly once and fails the job loudly (a diagnosable `JobOutcome::faulted`) instead of looping if it isn't `done()` yet — safe by construction, not merely by luck of today's deployment shape, reusing `AsyncMutex`/`channel<T>`'s own already-proven "destroyed while parked = safe cancel" contract (ADR-017). New `tests/test_rt_thread_pool.cpp` T6 proves the fault fires correctly AND that the mutex is unharmed afterward for later legitimate use. Full suite 194/194 (`ctest -LE live-network`), zero regressions. |
