Do Findings 1 (content/semantic routing) and 3 (sticky/session-scoped tier pinning) hold up, and does the draft's own design for them survive independent red-teaming? | **Proposed — corrected, implemented, and proven; awaiting project-owner judgment.** An independent red-team pass found two real MUST-FIX problems in the first design memo: (1) the draft's I5 claim ("the base router needs zero new recording plumbing") only held by accident — a `SelectFn` seeing the full `EffectContext` could read the non-replay-stable `EffectContext::deadline`; fixed by narrowing `SelectFn` to `(ChatRequest const&) -> size_t`, no `EffectContext` access at all, making I5-purity a real `static_assert`-checked structural property. (2) `RoutingModelCallGateway::capabilities()` reporting only Route 0's capabilities (matching `ModelCallGateway`'s own "primary only" precedent) would silently break capability-based content routing, the routing use case genuinely implied — fixed by documenting this type as scoped to capability-equivalent Routes only (confirmed both of issue #16's own named use cases are exactly this shape), with heterogeneous-capability routing named as real, separate, unbuilt work needing `AgentSession`'s own pre-dispatch gates to become router-aware. New `include/agentengine/core/routing_model_call_gateway.hpp` (`RoutingModelCallGateway<SelectFn, Routes...>`, a third `ModelCallGatewayLike` conformer; router-wraps-failover composition proven, not just claimed), new `ChatResponse::route_index` field (orthogonal to `fallback_tier`), sticky tier pinning added to `ModelCallGateway<Primary, Fallback...>` itself for both `call()` and `call_stream()` (the latter needed threading a `tier_index` parameter into `stream_attempt_with_retry` to disambiguate its previously-overloaded `nullopt` return, a real subtlety found by direct code reading). Confirmed by direct read, not re-assumed: `AgentSessionRecord`/`fork_from()` already never touch `chat_client_`, settling the draft's own flagged-not-verified open question that sticky state won't survive session restore/fork — a pre-existing gap, not a new one. Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): new `tests/test_routing_model_call_gateway.cpp` (18/18 checks) and `tests/test_model_call_gateway.cpp` G12-G15 (25 new checks, 71/71 total), plus a `route_index` round-trip check added to `test_chat_recording_codec`. Full project rebuild and the full `ctest` suite: zero regressions (296 tests, the one pre-existing failure being the long-documented, unrelated `test_reference_agent_task_corpus` matplotlib/pandas environment gap — see ADR-081 et al.). Explicitly does NOT build the draft's own embedding-based `SemanticRoutingModelCallGateway`, does NOT make `AgentSession`'s capability gates router-aware, does NOT give `RoutingModelCallGateway` a `call_stream()` (named residual matching `MiddlewareModelCallGateway`'s own existing gap), and does NOT solve sticky mode's "never re-probes a more-preferred tier" limitation (proven to behave exactly this way by test G15, not merely asserted) or the tier-breaker staleness that compounds it. A follow-up independent code-review pass (fresh rebuild, fresh test runs, not trusting this session's own prior output) confirmed every claim above by direct re-derivation and found one further real, pre-existing gap this ADR names but does not fix: `AgentSession`'s streaming-reconstruction path (`detail::drain_streaming_response`) rebuilds `ChatResponse` via a bare 2-arg aggregate that silently resets `fallback_tier`/`route_index` to 0, so a streamed, gateway-routed turn's externally-visible response can't show which tier/Route actually answered even though the gateway tracks it correctly internally (§4a). |
| [149](ADR-149-magentic-workflow-convenience-api.md) | GitHub issue #28: can a Magentic-shaped workflow convenience API (manager/participant builder, stall/reset safety valve, typed plan-signoff HITL, checkpoint-persistence packaging, transcript reconstruction) be built as pure additive sugar over already-proven, non-concurrent primitives, without widening any capability/authority boundary? | **Proposed — implemented, independently red-teamed TWICE (design draft, then implementation), all evidence executed and passing, pending project-owner sign-off.** Red-team round 1 (design draft, before code existed) found 3 of its own 6 named punch-list items had a concrete defect plus 3 unnamed MUST-FIX problems, all fixed before code: `stalled` narrowed to one host-designated `designated_stall_reporter`; `require_plan_signoff()`'s port rewired to share the graph's own `<TaskMsg,ReportMsg>` type pair; `resume_or_start()` fails closed on an agent-kind executor without explicit `acknowledge_agent_history_reset`; `stall_streak_`/`resets_used_` added to `RunStateRecord` (an unlimited-stall-budget checkpoint/resume bypass otherwise); issue #28 item 6 honestly rescoped to `latest_outputs_of()` (most-recent-per-executor, not multi-visit history — deferred to a future ADR/#29). Implementation itself then found 3 more real defects past that red-team pass: a type-parameterization bug that would have made the builder unable to compile with two genuinely distinct message types; a stall-streak reset bug that silently defeated `max_stalls` for a normal manager/participant alternation; a pre-existing, unrelated `FileSessionStore` defect (Windows can't open a path containing `:`, but `WorkflowSupervisor::run_id()` always contains one, so disk-backed workflow checkpointing was silently broken on Windows) — fixed via `sanitize_for_filesystem()`, verified against the full pre-existing `test_rt_session_store.cpp` suite (43 checks, zero regressions). **Red-team round 2 (post-implementation, independently re-ran every test itself rather than trusting exit codes)** found all 15 of the ADR's falsifiable claims genuinely reproduce, PLUS one more real MUST-FIX bug: the stall-check loop took only the FIRST of two concurrent same-round deliveries to a non-`agent`-kind `designated_stall_reporter` and silently discarded a genuine `stalled=true` on the second (proven via a written-and-run standalone repro) — fixed to OR-aggregate `stalled` across every delivery to the reporter's index that round. Plus 3 lower-severity residuals, all fixed: a latent ODR risk from 3 files declaring `AE_WORKFLOW_MESSAGE`-annotated types with the same name but different strings (fixed by unifying the strings, matching this codebase's own pre-existing `Question`/`Draft`/`Verdict` precedent — an anonymous-namespace fix was tried first and does not compile, MSVC C2888, since an explicit specialization must be declared in a namespace enclosing the primary template's); `resume_or_start()`'s fail-closed guard widened to also cover `sub_workflow`-kind, not just `agent`-kind (currently inert since `sub_workflow` can't execute at all — issue #33 — but the guard's shape now won't need a second fix once it can); and the ADR's own claim that the live example "builds" turned out to be an untested assumption (no `.vcxproj` for it existed in `build-https` before the audit) that happened to be true once actually checked, surfacing one real, previously-unseen `C4244` narrowing warning, now cast away. Every fix re-verified: 4 new test binaries (6 new regression-proof scenarios across the two rounds' findings) all `ALL PASS`, every pre-existing `workflow_supervisor`-family test plus `test_rt_session_store` still zero-regression, `examples/19_magentic_builder_live.cpp` rebuilt clean under a REAL `build-https` config (not just the default), and a full clean project rebuild (MSVC, Visual Studio 18 2026, zero errors). Not claimed: issue #28 item 5 (typed live-event multiplexing) and full transcript reconstruction are deferred to a future ADR (also closing #29); the example's actual live-model run against a real API was still not executed (only its compile/skip-path was verified). NOTE: this ADR was drafted locally as "ADR-147", then renumbered to 148 after a rebase onto origin/main's own concurrent ADR-147 (task-scope containment corpus), then renumbered again to 149 after a SECOND rebase found origin/main had, in the meantime, also gained its own concurrent ADR-148 (model-call-gateway routing) — multiple sessions independently claiming the next free ADR number at the same time. Every in-file self-reference was updated to match both times. |
| [150](ADR-150-workflow-as-executor-adapter.md) | Can a whole built `Workflow` be reused as ONE ordinary participant inside another workflow's builder — no new `executor_kind`, no graph-level wiring — as pure additive sugar over `WorkflowSupervisor`, mirroring MAF's `workflow.as_agent()` in spirit? Started as an attempt at GitHub issue #35; a red-team pass found #35 is actually centered on a different, `ChatClient`-shaped adapter, so this was re-scoped and filed as its own issue, #36. | **Proposed — implemented, independently red-teamed once (design draft, before code existed), the single most severe finding adversarially verified post-fix, pending project-owner sign-off.** The first design draft's own concurrency claim ("`WorkflowSupervisor::run_mutex_` safely queues a second concurrent call") was traced by the red-team to be actively wrong: `run_mutex_` is an `AsyncMutex` whose contended path parks the awaiting coroutine's own handle, resumable only by the matching `unlock()`'s own trampoline — the adapter's own "resume until done" `drive()` loop has no way to honor that, and two deliveries to the same wrapped node in one outer round (ordinary topology for a `function`-kind node, no agent-kind-only dedup applies) would race on shared state. **Fixed with the adapter's own independent `std::mutex`, then adversarially verified**: temporarily removing the mutex guard and rebuilding reproduced a real segfault 3/3 runs, exactly at the new concurrency test; restored, the same test passes cleanly across 5 trials — proving the fix is load-bearing, not decorative. Two more MUST-FIX findings: fail-closed on a suspended inner run was understated as merely "disclosed" when it actually silently and permanently orphaned the inner workflow's open interaction the next call — fixed by refusing construction outright for any inner graph containing a `request_port` executor, eliminating the hazard structurally; and `WorkflowSupervisor` turns out to be immovable (embeds an `AsyncMutex`, whose deleted copy ops suppress the implicit move ctor too), so the original reference-only design invited a real dangling-reference trap for exactly its own headline "reusable, factory-built participant" use case — fixed by adding a `shared_ptr`-owning overload as the primary surface. New `include/agentengine/rt/workflow_as_executor.hpp` (`workflow_as_executor_body()`, both overloads), `tests/test_rt_workflow_as_executor.cpp` (11 falsifiable claims, all CORRECT, including the adversarial mutation proof), `examples/21_workflow_as_participant.cpp` — a whole inner `Workflow` wrapped and used as one `.participant()` inside an OUTER `MagenticWorkflowBuilder` graph (ADR-149's own convenience builder) alongside an ordinary function participant, run for real, `completed`. No existing engine file (`workflow_supervisor.hpp`, `graph.hpp`, `magentic.hpp`) was touched — genuinely additive. Full `cmake --build` (Debug, MSVC, Visual Studio 18 2026, zero errors, zero warnings under the newly-enforced `/WX`) and the full workflow-family `ctest` suite (24/24) both clean. Not claimed: does not close #35 itself (the `ChatClient`-shaped adapter — different mechanism, different open questions, left open); does not touch #33 (`sub_workflow`-as-graph-node, unrelated mechanism); no nested `request_port` proxying (named, unbuilt future work — refused outright today, not mishandled). |
| [151](ADR-151-delegated-agent-approval-policy.md) | GitHub issue #30: `PolicyDecider` (ADR-070) already receives `Principal::on_behalf_of`/`delegation_depth`, so a policy auto-approving a delegated/spawned agent's own `policy_driven` tool calls was fully expressible today with zero engine changes — but no reference implementation existed anywhere in this codebase, and `rt/agent_spawn.hpp`/`rt/agent_spawn_child_run.hpp` wired identity delegation but had no path to give a spawned child its own decider at all. Is the pattern real, and does a first design for it survive independent red-teaming? | **Proposed — corrected, implemented, and proven; awaiting project-owner judgment.** An independent red-team pass found two MUST-FIX problems in the first design memo: (1) the draft gated its fast path on `arguments_tainted`, but tracing every real call site that ever threads a live `PolicyDecider` (`rt/agent_session.hpp`'s suspend-for-approval pre-check and its one `invoke_tool()` call site, both via `core/tool_call_extraction.hpp`'s `tool_call_request_of()`) found `arguments_tainted` hard-coded `true`, unconditionally, for every genuinely model-originated call — the only kind of call that ever reaches a `policy_driven` decision at all; gating on it would have made the reference policy's delegation-awareness dead code in production. Fixed by dropping the taint gate entirely and naming the constant honestly in the new header's own file banner. (2) the draft's own claim that a spawned child's `require_approval` fallback "falls back to human review" was false: `run_child_agent_session()` drives a child synchronously to completion and destroys it before returning, so it can never genuinely suspend for a later human answer — with no `ApprovalDecider` also wired, `require_approval` silently degenerates to an outright denial. Fixed by propagating BOTH `ApprovalDecider` and `PolicyDecider` down to a spawned child (not `PolicyDecider` alone), and naming the residual explicitly in the new header, in `run_child_agent_session()`'s own comment, and in the new example. A should-fix from the same pass (per-target scoping, matching `child_token_budget`'s own established precedent, rather than a shared-per-provider knob) was also incorporated, simplifying the plumbing to two new `SpawnTargetDescriptor` fields with no new function parameters needed anywhere. New `include/agentengine/trust/delegated_approval_policy.hpp` (`trust::approve_delegated_calls(max_depth)`, never returns `auto_deny`), two new opt-in fields each on `SpawnTargetDescriptor` and `ChildSpawnRequest`, `run_child_agent_session()`'s new wiring, new `examples/24_delegated_agent_approval.cpp` (both wiring points shown side by side, deliberately contrasted). Extended `tests/test_agent_tool_invocation.cpp` (case 6, 4 sub-cases through the real `invoke_agent_tool()` entry point) and `tests/test_rt_agent_spawn.cpp` (T8-T9, through the real `perform_agent_spawn()`/`AgentSpawnTool` path with a genuinely scripted child turn loop, not a hand-built `ToolCallRequest`). Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): all new/extended tests and the new example pass; full project rebuild and the full `ctest` suite: 302/303 passed, the one failure being the same pre-existing, unrelated `test_reference_agent_task_corpus` matplotlib/pandas environment gap ADR-148 already documents. A follow-up independent code-review pass (fresh session, no prior context) re-derived every claim in this ADR directly from the on-disk code, re-ran the full build/test/lint sequence itself, and found zero discrepancies — its one note (a missed `std::move()` on the two new decider fields, a micro-optimization) was applied. NOTE: drafted locally as ADR-149 / example 19, then renumbered to ADR-150 / example 21 after a rebase found origin/main had concurrently gained its own unrelated ADR-149 (Magentic workflow, issue #28) and examples 19-20, then renumbered again to ADR-151 / example 24 after a SECOND rebase found origin/main had, in the meantime, ALSO concurrently gained its own unrelated ADR-150 (workflow-as-executor adapter, above) and examples 21-23 — the same multiple-sessions-racing-on-the-next-free-number pattern ADR-149's own note above already records, now a third time in the same short window. Every in-file self-reference was updated to match each time. Explicitly does NOT fix the spawned-child suspend gap (named, not new), does NOT touch `text_derived`/`Tainted<T>`/the middleware declassifying closure, and does NOT change what a delegated caller could already do (capability bind still runs unconditionally before this policy is ever consulted). |
| [152](ADR-152-workflow-event-streaming.md) | GitHub issue #29: `WorkflowSupervisor`'s only observability surface (`enable_live_view()`) is a coarse once-per-superstep summary; `AgentSession::enable_event_stream()`'s already-rich per-token event vocabulary never reaches the workflow layer. Project-owner direction: every event any node produces must be yielded LIVE, the instant it happens, multiplexed across however many nodes are concurrently in flight in one round — not a richer summary. Is a genuinely-fresh architecture warranted over patching the first design draft's own approach into working? | **Proposed — implemented, red-teamed twice (design draft before code existed, plus two structural gaps found and closed during implementation), the single most severe claim adversarially verified post-fix, 35/35 new test checks passing, pending full regression `ctest` and project-owner sign-off.** The first draft's own central question — is concurrent multi-thread `push()` into ONE `rt::channel`-backed `stream_producer<T>` memory-safe? — was confirmed YES empirically (a scratchpad stress test, ~2.7M pushes, zero corruption). That confirmation turned out not to matter: the red team found the real hazard was never memory safety but LIVENESS — `channel_producer<T,E>::push()` deliberately blocks the calling OS thread under backpressure, correct for a dedicated I/O thread, wrong for a `ThreadPool` worker thread also needed for real workflow compute; enough concurrently-streaming nodes in one fan-out round, each blocked on a lagging UI consumer, could exhaust the pool and stall actual workflow execution — unacceptable for a purely-additive observability feature. **Fixed with a genuinely fresh mechanism, not a patch**: a new, purpose-built, non-blocking MPSC primitive (`workflow::multiplex_sink<T>`) for the per-node multiplexed bucket only (an agent-kind node's real per-token deltas, or a function-kind moderator's own forwarded `chat_stream()` deltas) — `push()` never blocks, drops silently on overflow with a diagnostic counter; the structural bucket (routing/lifecycle/checkpoint decisions) keeps the original single-writer channel design, which was never at risk. The red team also found the first draft's own named "safe fallback" (buffer privately, drain "between yield points") was not a fix at all — `execute()`'s round-collection loop has no yield points until every job in the round finishes, so that fallback would have silently degraded to the exact round-boundary batching issue #29 was filed to eliminate; recorded so it's never mistaken for a lesser-but-working alternative. Two more real (not hypothetical) collisions found and fixed: reusing `EffectContext::report_progress` for the bridge would silently reinterpret a `function`-kind body's own direct `Tool<>::invoke()` progress as a workflow event — fixed with two new dedicated fields (`agent_turn_sink`/`moderator_delta_sink`); bridging via a second `AgentSession::enable_event_stream()` call would silently evict any consumer an app already attached directly to that session (the channel's own single-consumer contract) — fixed with an independent plain callback tap (`set_run_event_tap()`), verified directly: both a direct tap and the workflow bridge, wired simultaneously on the same session, observe the IDENTICAL live `RunEvent` sequence, neither evicting the other. Implementation itself then found 2 more structural gaps past that red-team pass: the agent-kind bridge turned out simpler than planned — a synchronous tap fired from inside `emit_run_event_for()` needed no restructuring of `agent_session_as_executor_body()`'s existing concurrency-fragile `drive()` loop at all, a genuine simplification over the draft's own "interleave drain with resume()" plan; and every non-`execute()` `WorkflowResult` return site (`run_workflow`/`resume_workflow`/`continue_workflow`'s own early-return branches) needed its own explicit terminal event, since `finish()` alone never sees those paths. New `include/agentengine/workflow/workflow_event.hpp` (`WorkflowEvent`/`workflow_event_kind`/`WorkflowEventStream`), `include/agentengine/workflow/multiplex_sink.hpp`, `tests/test_rt_workflow_event_stream.cpp` (9 falsifiable claims, W1-W9, all CORRECT), `examples/25_workflow_event_stream_live.cpp` (a real concurrent 3-way fan-out/fan-in graph, 9 live streaming events observed, one aggregated `fan_out_dispatched`, one aggregated `fan_in_aggregated`). **The central liveness claim (W9) was adversarially verified, not just reasoned about**: with `multiplex_sink::push()` temporarily mutated to spin-block on a full queue, the exact same test hung and was killed by a 15-second process timeout, never reaching W9's own output — reverted, re-verified clean (all 35 checks pass) before this ADR was written, the same discipline ADR-150's own mutation test used. Not claimed: no `Custom.type_id` standardization for generic tool-progress UI rendering (issue #29's own Group F, separate follow-up); does not touch the backgrounded-tool progress gap (ADR-060 §4's own named residual); no fan-out to multiple simultaneous `enable_event_stream()` consumers (same single-consumer convention `enable_live_view()` already uses); `moderator_stream_delta` forwarding is opt-in only, no engine enforcement that any real body streams (I3). The bridge was also proven against a REAL model (OpenRouter, `openai/gpt-4o-mini`): `examples/26_workflow_event_stream_live_openrouter.cpp` observed 39 genuine per-token `model_delta` events live through the full chain, reassembling byte-for-byte into the model's real answer. Full repo-wide `ctest`: 308/309 passed (the one failure the same long-documented, pre-existing `test_reference_agent_task_corpus` matplotlib/pandas environment gap ADR-149's own row already names — not a regression). Getting to that genuinely clean build also surfaced and fixed 4 small, pre-existing, unrelated latent warnings-as-errors in other files (an unreachable-code false-unreachability in `test_rt_multi_agent.cpp`'s own throwing-thunk test, a parameter-shadows-member in `skill_provider.hpp`, and two `int`/`wstring`-to-`char`/`string` narrowing conversions in `examples/17_planner_live.cpp` and `tools/cli_chat.cpp`) — each fixed at its real site (never a `#pragma` suppression, which this project's own `pal/env.hpp` precedent explicitly forbids), disclosed in ADR-152 §7, not silently folded in. NOTE: this ADR was drafted locally as "ADR-151" and its first example as `examples/24_*`, then renumbered to 152/`examples/25_*` after a rebase found origin/main had, in the meantime, independently claimed ADR-151 and example 24 for a concurrent, unrelated change (issue #30's delegated approval policy) — the same multi-session numbering collision ADR-149's own NOTE already documents happening twice before. |
| [153](ADR-153-agent-memory-codeact-bridging.md) | GitHub issue #31's own audit comment: `MemoryProvider`'s `recall` tool (026 §5's `agent.tools.recall`) is real and tested but reachable only through the native tool-calling loop, never `ToolBridgeConfig`, and `/memory`'s raw-file-read half has no mount wiring at all — can a host reach both from a CodeAct sandbox using only already-real primitives, without a new ambient-authority path? | **Judged (2026-08-31, project-owner sign-off).** Red-teamed by actually building and running the design as sketched, not just reading it — both findings below were CAUGHT BY A FAILING TEST. Finding 1 (must-fix): the original `make_recall_tool_descriptor()` closure captured its `cap::FsRead` by value from the provider's own construction and never set `ToolDescriptor::capability_ceiling`, so bridging it into a `ToolBridgeConfig` with an EMPTY `capabilities` list still let the sandbox read memory — `invoke_tool()`'s step 4/7 authorize+bind never even ran, reaching straight through to the native session's own authority, the exact hazard `tool_bridge.hpp`'s own header warns against. Fixed by setting `capability_ceiling = {Capability{read_cap_}}`, gating both callers identically (a no-op behavior change for the existing native path, confirmed by `test_memory_provider.cpp` unchanged). Finding 2 (must-fix): the new `materialize_memory_mount()` (mirroring `materialize_skill_mounts()`'s shape) initially reused `memory_mount_id(principal)` ("memory:<tenant>:<id>") directly as the host subdirectory name — a string safe only as an opaque capability-matching key, not a filesystem path segment; this failed with a real Windows `ERROR_INVALID_NAME`-class error, observed, not theorized. Fixed by taking a separate, host-chosen `mount_name` presentation token (e.g. "memory") for the directory/guest-visible name, leaving the real `mount_id` as the (unchanged) authority-check key `materialize_mount()` itself still verifies internally. New `include/agentengine/core/memory_provider.hpp` change (`make_recall_tool_descriptor()` now public + capability-gated), new `src/backends/native_jail/memory_mount_materializer.hpp`, new `tests/test_memory_codeact_bridging.cpp` (12 checks, all pass, including both must-fix findings' own regression checks observed to fail pre-fix and pass post-fix). Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): the new target plus every existing test touching `MemoryProvider`/`ToolBridgeConfig` (`test_memory_provider`, `test_tool_bridge`, `test_memory_ranking_formula`, `test_memory_worktree`, `test_skill_mount_materializer`) all pass, no regression. Explicitly does NOT touch `agent.notes`' write-back (ADR-156, a separate follow-on reusing this ADR's own mount), does not add any automatic `ContextContribution`-to-sandbox wiring (a host must still explicitly opt in), and does not prove a full running `NativeJailBackend` worker end to end (proves `bridge_tool_call()`/`materialize_memory_mount()` directly, the same layer their own sibling tests already prove at). NOTE: drafted locally as ADR-152, renumbered to ADR-153 after a rebase found origin/main had, in the meantime, independently claimed ADR-152 for a concurrent, unrelated change (issue #29's workflow event streaming, above) — the same multi-session numbering collision this row's own predecessor rows already document happening repeatedly. |
| [154](ADR-154-agent-output-codeact-module.md) | GitHub issue #39: `agent.output` (026 §5, "Emit structured output conforming to the run's schema", zero capability) is spec'd and manifest-registered but has zero implementation. Can a script declare a structured result mid-run, using only the ALREADY-EXISTING final `exec_response` frame — no new frame type, no touching `exec_session()`'s mid-loop dispatch — without conflating it with ADR-058's genuinely separate run-level `structured_output_json`? | **Judged (2026-08-31, project-owner sign-off).** `agent.output.set(value)` (matching ADR-058 §2's own naming): a plain, synchronous `_ae_internal.set_output(json_text)` write into a per-run-reset global, threaded through three new fields (one per existing pipe layer: `WorkerExecResult` → the exec_response wire frame → `ExecOutcome`) all the way to the host — reusing the SAME frame `result_repr`/`ask_prompt` already ride on, deliberately avoiding `agent.progress`'s (ADR-155) harder mid-execution-frame problem entirely, since a value only needs to be read once the script ends. New opt-in `expose_agent_output` (matching `expose_agent_ask`'s exact precedent — zero capability, still opt-in). New `src/backends/native_jail/agent_output_codegen.hpp`, `Internal_set_output` in `python_worker_mediation.cpp`, new `tests/test_agent_output_codegen.cpp` (12 checks, run against the REAL built `agentengine_python_worker.exe`, not a stub) proving: absent without opt-in (loud `AttributeError`, matching 026 §5a's own "ungranted module is simply absent" design), a real round trip once opted in, the call does NOT abort/suspend the script (unlike `agent.ask`), last-call-wins on a second call in the same script, and — a genuine regression check, not a formality — no cross-execution leak of a PREVIOUS call's value onto a later script that never calls it. Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): the new target plus the full related native-jail/python-worker suite (7 targets, including ADR-057's own `agent.ask` end-to-end test, which shares the identical wire frame this ADR extended) all pass, no regression. Explicitly does NOT populate `rt::AgentSession::structured_output_json` (ADR-058's own, separate, model-response-driven field) — wiring a tool's own result into an early-exit-the-round-loop decision is named as a materially bigger, separate follow-on, not attempted here — and does not validate the emitted value against any schema (no generic JSON-Schema validator exists anywhere in this codebase, per ADR-058 §1's own finding). |
| [155](ADR-155-agent-progress-codeact-module.md) | GitHub issue #31's own three-piece gap (`agent.progress` spec'd, manifest-registered, zero implementation) — the issue's own sketch warns that adding the Python/worker-side pieces without a matching host-side frame-type branch would crash the worker outright. Can the mechanism reach ADR-060's already-real `report_progress` channel from CodeAct without needing a new wire frame type at all, closing that exact landmine by construction? | **Judged (2026-08-31, project-owner sign-off).** Deliberately diverges from issue #31's own "new frame type" sketch: reuses the EXISTING `worker_query`/`worker_query_response` envelope as one more KIND (`kQueryProgress`) rather than inventing a new frame TYPE — `exec_session()`'s frame-loop dispatch needs no new branch at all (a new `kind` is just one more `else if` inside the already-existing `dispatch_worker_query()`), so the fail-closed `terminate_worker()` fallback issue #31 itself warns about has no new frame type to ever fail to recognize in the first place, and RT1 Finding 1's exec_seq-mismatch check (already uniform across every `worker_query` kind) applies for free. Reaching `EffectContext::report_progress` needed ZERO `AgentSession`-side changes — `execute_code` is itself an ordinary tool call, so `agent_session.hpp`'s own three ADR-060 brackets have already bound the live, `force_tainted()`-wrapping closure by the time `exec_session()`'s loop runs; this is exactly ADR-060 §3's own named, deliberately-deferred follow-on. New `kQueryProgress` (`mediated_python_worker_protocol.hpp`), `Internal_report_progress` (`python_worker_mediation.cpp`, a synchronous `g_query_fn` round trip — the script does not abort/suspend, proven by stdout ordering), new `src/backends/native_jail/agent_progress_codegen.hpp`, new opt-in `expose_agent_progress` (matching `expose_agent_ask`/`expose_agent_output`'s exact precedent), new `tests/test_agent_progress_codegen.cpp` (10 checks against the REAL built worker) proving: absent without opt-in, a real multi-call streaming round trip (unlike `agent.output`'s last-call-wins, each call is its own event, in order), the worker is NOT crashed by any call, and the SAME worker handles a later, unrelated call normally afterward (ruling out latent damage). Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): the new target plus the full related suite (7 targets, including ADR-060's own native `report_progress` test and ADR-057's own `agent.ask` test) all pass, no regression. Explicitly out of scope: structured/typed payloads (plain string only, matching ADR-060 §3), `Backgroundable` delivery, and real MCP/AG-UI projection through this specific path — the same residuals ADR-060 §7 already named. |
| [156](ADR-156-agent-notes-write-back.md) | GitHub issue #40: `agent.notes` has ZERO implementation — no mount, no file-write-to-MemoryItem conversion mechanism, nothing (unlike `agent.progress`/`agent.output`, 026 §5 frames `agent.notes` as ordinary file access, not a Python module needing a codegen bootstrap). Can a script's ordinary write become a durable `AgentAuthored` `MemoryItem`, reusing `agent.files`' already-real generic write mechanism for the write itself, without the staging area colliding with the structural storage `MemoryProvider`'s own extraction path already writes to? | **Judged (2026-08-31, project-owner sign-off).** The "ordinary write" half needed no new code at all — `agent.files`' `open()`/`file_write` (`agent_files_data_codegen.hpp`) already works against any mounted path generically. First design draft scoped the guest-writable staging area as a `notes-inbox` SUBTREE of the SAME ref `agent.memory`'s structural mount uses — wrong, caught by `test_memory_notes_write_back.cpp`'s own R2 check FAILING with a real, observed `json.malformed_number: expected a digit`: `rank_memory_items()`/`list_memory_items()` walk the ENTIRE ref from the structural mount's own `subtree_path=""` and try to JSON-decode every leaf as a `MemoryItem` record, choking on raw note text sitting as a sibling of the real structural JSON records. Fixed with a genuinely SEPARATE ref (`memory_notes_inbox_ref_name()`, its own bootstrap mirroring `ensure_memory_worktree()`'s exact shape) — closing the collision by construction, not by a subtree-scoping convention a future caller could get wrong. New `src/backends/native_jail/memory_notes_materializer.hpp` (`prepare_memory_notes_mount()`, `harvest_memory_notes()` — reusing `harvest_mount()`, the exact primitive ADR-153 §7 already named as this write-back mechanism's own residual), new `tests/test_memory_notes_write_back.cpp` (18 checks) proving: a staged file becomes a real, durable, `recall`-discoverable `AgentAuthored` `MemoryItem` with real run_id/turn_id attribution; an empty staging dir produces no false positives; the reserved-name collision guard fails closed; and — the must-fix regression check — a later turn's harvest never re-ingests an earlier turn's own structural records. Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): the new target plus the full memory-subsystem suite (8 targets, including ADR-153's own `test_memory_codeact_bridging`) all pass, no regression. Named residual, not silently assumed stronger than it is: `cap::FsWrite`/`FsRead` match on bare `mount_id` only, so the isolation between the inbox and structural mounts is a CODE-STRUCTURE guarantee (the sandbox's own `mount_roots` is never folded from the structural mount at all) rather than a capability-level one. Explicitly does not touch reading prior notes back (that's `agent.memory`/ADR-153's own job) or prove a full running `NativeJailBackend` worker end to end. A follow-up code-review pass found and fixed 4 more issues before this ADR was committed: `harvest_memory_notes()` was discarding already-durably-committed `MemoryItem`s when a LATER item's write failed, aborting the whole batch instead of best-effort per item (fixed); a missing explicit `<algorithm>` include relying on a transitive pull-in via `capability.hpp` (fixed); and duplicated reserved-mount-id-check / ref-bootstrap logic between this ADR's files and ADR-153's, now shared via `reserve_and_create_mount_dir()`/`ensure_worktree_ref()`. |
| [157](ADR-157-sub-workflow-nested-request-port.md) | GitHub issues #33 (`executor_kind::sub_workflow` declared but unconditionally refused, no runtime bridge) and #38 (`workflow_as_executor_body()`, ADR-150, deliberately refuses to wrap an inner graph that itself suspends on its own `request_port` — `ExecutorBody`'s one-shot synchronous contract cannot express suspension). Closed together as one mechanism: can a `sub_workflow`-kind node's own inner suspension surface outward through the OUTER graph's existing `Interaction`/`request_port` machinery, with the eventual response routed back down into the specific paused inner run, mirroring MAF's `WorkflowHostExecutor`? | **Proposed — implemented, red-teamed once (design draft before any code existed, revised against every finding), the single most severe claim adversarially verified — including catching and fixing a false-negative in the FIRST verification attempt before trusting a clean result — 22/22 new test checks passing, pending project-owner sign-off.** The first draft's own mechanism (extending `OpenPort` with an `inner_interaction_id` field) was found actively wrong, not just inelegant: `resume_workflow()` writes the raw human answer into `OpenPort::response` UNCONDITIONALLY before any marker field could be checked, so a naive implementation would have folded a nested question's raw human answer as if it were the sub-workflow's own completed output — and this exact confusion would have survived a checkpoint/restore cycle undetected, producing precisely the "quietly reinterpreted graph" hazard `graph.hpp`'s own design principle warns against. **Fixed by not touching `OpenPort` at all**: a fully separate `pending_sub_workflows_` tracker holds suspended state, and an `OpenPort` is only ever constructed AFTER the derived value is already computed, never mutated into after the fact — which also makes checkpoint-restore fail closed for free (a restored instance's `pending_sub_workflows_` starts empty, so resuming a stale nested interaction_id falls through to `workflow_status::invalid`, never silent misrouting). The I1 concurrency hazard (two sub_workflow deliveries to the SAME bound inner supervisor in one round — the identical class of hazard ADR-150's own red team found) was fixed by generalizing the already-proven OQ-19 same-round-duplicate-delivery quarantine (previously agent-kind only) rather than inventing a new mutex. **The adversarial verification of that fix caught a real false-negative in its own first attempt**: the first concurrency test used `fan_in` edges to converge two deliveries onto the same sub_workflow node, and 55 trials (including a deliberately widened 15ms race window) showed zero crashes — not because the fix worked, but because `route_from()`'s `fan_in` handling MERGES converging replies into ONE delivery entry before dispatch, so the "duplicate" never reached the quarantine at all (confirmed via atomic-counter instrumentation: zero genuine concurrent overlap ever occurred). Rebuilt with `direct` edges (which do not merge, matching the quarantine's own real target case) — the quarantine-bypass mutation then segfaulted 20/20 runs, and the restored fix passed cleanly 15/15 with zero overlap, confirming the fix is genuinely load-bearing. A separate, independently-found bug (not from the red team): the round loop's own bottom-of-round suspend check never consulted `pending_sub_workflows_`, so a round whose ONLY open thing was a freshly-suspended nested interaction silently fell through to reporting `completed` instead of `suspended` — fixed by extending that one check. Also fixed: a round-abort-orphan hazard (a sub_workflow suspending in the same round a DIFFERENT executor's routing fails must not silently drop the live nested interaction) by making `open_interactions()`'s population in `finish()` unconditional rather than gated behind `status == suspended`. New `include/agentengine/rt/workflow_supervisor.hpp` additions (`bind_sub_workflow()`, `pending_sub_workflows_`, `run_sub_workflow_job()`), `include/agentengine/workflow/graph.hpp`'s `check_workflow_executable()` narrowed to mirror agent-kind's own two-layer accept-then-structurally-verify shape, `tests/test_rt_workflow_sub_workflow.cpp` (S1-S8, all CORRECT), `examples/27_sub_workflow_nested_request_port.cpp` (an approval pipeline nested inside a publishing workflow, mirroring MAF's `sub_workflow_request_interception.py`). One pre-existing test (`test_workflow_agent_executor_gate`'s G6) asserted the OLD "sub_workflow refused unconditionally" contract this ADR deliberately changes — updated to assert the new, intentional contract. Full repo-wide `ctest`: 310/312 passed, both failures pre-existing and confirmed unrelated (the same matplotlib/pandas gap, plus `test_rt_spawn_cost_budget` confirmed genuinely flaky by direct rerun — 2/3 clean, 1/3 fails under real 8-thread contention in a subsystem this pass never touches). Not claimed: does not touch `workflow_as_executor_body()` (ADR-150) at all; does not forward an inner graph's own ADR-152 multiplexed events (agent-kind/moderator streaming) to the outer's event stream, only the structural suspend/resume signal; does not redesign `ThreadPool` sharing across nested instances (only a bounded 2-level/3-wide-fan-out shape is tested); does not enforce (only documents) that one `inner` instance must be bound to at most one executor_index; does not attempt or claim 3+ level nesting safety. **Follow-up (same session, two passes, issue #42's own tracked residuals): (1) closed the bind-caller-contract gaps just disclosed above — a wrong-`executor_id`-kind bind and a duplicate-`inner`-instance bind are now both genuinely refused, not just documented (S9/S10); proved 3-level nesting actually works, not just extrapolated from the 2-level shape (S11). (2) Designed, red-teamed, implemented, and proven a `ThreadPool` resource-budgeting mechanism closing the "does not redesign ThreadPool sharing/ceilings" residual above: a static per-`WorkflowSupervisor` worker budget (`WorkflowSupervisor(worker_budget)`), a fail-closed `split_worker_budget()` helper (refuses rather than silently granting a starved child an unbounded default-sized pool), and a mandatory, automatically-tracked `kMaxNestingDepth` cap (=16) that makes the budget arithmetic a genuine ceiling at all. Proven both positively (a real nested tree's live OS thread count, measured via a new process-wide `live_worker_thread_count()` gauge, never exceeds its declared budget) and by mutation (temporarily forcing the constructor to ignore its budget parameter reproduced a real, measured ceiling breach — 21 live threads against a declared 7). The mutation test also caught a real false-negative in its own first attempt, independently reproducing the exact class of bug ADR-157's own Pass 2 found once already: a background sampler thread racing a fast synchronous run could take zero samples and silently pass regardless of the true peak, fixed by a deterministic direct sample instead (`ThreadPool` workers are created once at construction and live for the whole instance's lifetime, never per-round). Of the four originally-named residuals, only one remained open at that point: forwarding ADR-152's multiplexed events across nested runs (issue #42 item 3). **(3) That last residual is now also closed.** A THIRD independent red-team pass (fresh agent, zero prior context) reviewed the event-forwarding design BEFORE any code was written and found the first-drafted mechanism (bind-time/enable-time sink propagation, cascading recursively at `bind_sub_workflow()`/`enable_event_stream()` time) was genuinely wrong on two counts: it would have produced INCORRECT path values under this codebase's own real bottom-up nested-tree construction order (every real tree, including S8/S11, binds innermost-first — a bind-time-computed path can't reflect ancestor binds that happen later), and it would have introduced a genuine NEW cross-thread data race (`enable_event_stream()`'s own docs already bless being called more than once, and nothing serializes it against a bind-time cascade racing a live in-flight dispatch on another thread). **Fixed by redesigning before shipping, not patching after**: the mechanism now wires an inner's multiplexed sink and full-path prefix TRANSIENTLY, at dispatch time only, immediately before each nested `drive()` call and restored immediately after (`ScopedForwardedEventSink`, a new RAII guard) — closing both findings by construction (nothing is ever precomputed at bind/enable time; the one write always immediately precedes, on the calling thread, the one call whose own worker threads are the only readers) and making multi-level cascading automatic with zero recursive machinery. New `AgentTurn::path`/`ModeratorDelta::path` fields (`workflow_event.hpp`) carry the full lineage of sub_workflow executor_ids from outer to originating node. Proven across 4 dimensions plus a real mutation test: single-level forwarding and path tagging (W10), 3-level full-path tagging (W11), genuine LIVENESS — a nested event observed on the outer's stream while the inner run is still verifiably in progress, via a deliberately-blocked inner node and a structurally-guaranteed (not timing-raced) assertion (W12), bind/enable order independence (W13a/b), and a mutation proof (temporarily disabling the wiring reproduced 8+ real check failures, reverted, re-confirmed clean). `test_rt_workflow_event_stream.cpp` grew from 28 to 53/53 passing checks. **All four of ADR-157's originally-named residuals are now closed — issue #42 is fully closed.** Full repo-wide `ctest`: 315/317 passing (both failures pre-existing and confirmed unrelated — the same matplotlib/pandas gap, plus `test_rt_spawn_cost_budget` confirmed clean 3/3 on direct rerun, the same already-documented flaky concurrency assertion). |
| [158](ADR-158-tool-concurrency-exclusivity-policy.md) | GitHub issue #41 (originally sourced from a comparative review of Microsoft Agent Framework's own recent, still-unresolved struggle with per-tool concurrency: Python shipped unconditional concurrency and got races, .NET shipped unconditional sequencing and lost parallelism, and MAF's own `concurrency_group` exclusivity mechanism was deferred as too hard to design quickly). `Parallelizable` (006 §5) is whole-batch, all-or-nothing, with no vocabulary for "safe alongside most tools, never alongside tool X" — does AgentEngine need a declared per-tool exclusivity dimension before 006 §8 G4's parallel-batch scheduler is built, and does a first design for it survive independent red-teaming? | **Proposed — corrected, implemented, and proven; awaiting project-owner judgment.** An independent red-team pass found three MUST-FIX problems in the first design draft: (1) the draft's `ExclusivityGroup<Tag>` was proposed layered ON TOP OF `Parallelizable`, silently redefining `Parallelizable`'s own unconditional ("safe with literally everyone") meaning while the draft claimed zero change — fixed by making `ExclusivityGroup<Name>` a DISTINCT, alternative concurrency claim a tool declares INSTEAD OF `Parallelizable`, never both, with 006 §5 honestly named as needing an amendment (a second, named way to opt into a parallel batch), not a no-op. (2) A direct in-document self-contradiction about how the new mechanism composes with `Background<max_concurrent>` — fixed by actually resolving it: the two mechanisms govern disjoint call shapes (an ordinary in-batch call vs. an explicitly detached background task) and need no interaction rule. (3) The literal first sketch (`ExclusivityGroup<Tag>`, an arbitrary type parameter) would have needed `typeid`-based type erasure to correlate independently-compiled tools' groups at runtime — RTTI, which `CONVENTIONS.md` forbids for policy on the hot path; fixed by re-specifying as `ExclusivityGroup<agentengine::fixed_string Name>`, matching the ALREADY-SHIPPED `cap::decl::ToolCall<Name>` precedent the first draft had missed entirely. Four SHOULD-FIX weaknesses also incorporated (non-transitivity named as a residual; multi-declaration resolved as a compile error rather than silent last-wins; Option B's rejection sharpened to ADR-070 property 4's taint-isolation rule rather than a vaguer "structural size" objection; the MAF safety-incident framing softened since AgentEngine has no live scheduler yet to reproduce that specific harm). New `ExclusivityGroup<Name>` policy tag, `Tool<Derived,Policies...>::declared_exclusivity_group()`, and two `static_assert` guardrails (`core/tool.hpp`); new `ToolDescriptor::exclusivity_group` populated by both tool-descriptor factories (`core/tool_pipeline.hpp`) — matching `Parallelizable`'s own existing shape, a real, tested declaration with **zero pipeline logic yet** (006 §8 G4's scheduler itself remains deferred, unbuilt by this ADR). Implementation itself then found one more real defect past the design-time red-team: the two static_asserts, first written as a raw arithmetic fold (`(static_cast<int>(pack_expr) + ...)`), made MSVC (Visual Studio 18) reject the pre-existing, empty-`Policies...` instantiation `Tool<agentengine::rt::ScheduleWakeupTool>` with C3520 — a genuine build break caught by this ADR's own full-rebuild step, fixed by rewriting both as immediately-invoked lambdas over a comma-operator fold, matching this struct's own pre-existing accessor idiom. New `tests/test_tool_exclusivity_group.cpp` (10 checks, through the REAL `Tool<>`/`make_tool_descriptor<>()` path) proving: `Parallelizable` alone never populates the new field (the MUST-FIX 1 regression check); a declared group's name round-trips through both the compile-time accessor and the runtime descriptor; two independently-declared tool TYPES sharing one group name report an identical value (the real point of the `fixed_string` NTTP — correlation by name, not by C++ type identity); distinct groups never collide. New `tests/compile_fail/tool_rejects_parallelizable_and_exclusivity_group.cpp` and `tests/compile_fail/tool_rejects_duplicate_exclusivity_group.cpp`, wired via `tests/CMakeLists.txt`'s existing `try_compile()` idiom, both proven to fail to compile as required — no separate positive-control file needed, following the ADR-102 §3 C1 gate's own precedent (the real runtime test already exercises every legitimate shape). Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC, Visual Studio 18): zero compile errors, zero new warnings, all pre-existing compile-fail gates still print OK. Full repo-wide `ctest -C Debug`: **253/253 tests passed, 0 failed** (2 pre-existing, unrelated Windows-only skips), zero regression. `python tools/naming_lint.py`: clean, no new suppressions needed beyond `ExclusivityGroup`'s own `ae-naming-lint: allow` comment (same convention as `Parallelizable`/`Backgroundable`). Explicitly does NOT build 006 §8 G4's parallel-batch scheduler (the new descriptor field is read by no dispatch path yet, exactly like `Parallelizable` itself); does NOT resolve determinism-under-I5, remote/MCP-tool defaults, or the non-transitivity limitation (all three explicitly deferred to a follow-on ADR once G4 itself is built); does NOT touch `PolicyDecider`'s signature (Option B stays rejected); and does NOT edit 006 §5's own prose yet (a named, small remaining copy-editing step to bring the RFC text in line with this ADR, not performed silently here). A follow-up independent code-review pass (fresh session, no prior context) re-derived every evidence claim directly from the on-disk code and re-ran the full configure/build/`ctest`/`naming_lint.py` sequence itself from scratch — zero discrepancies found. NOTE: drafted and implemented locally as "ADR-157", renumbered to ADR-158 after a rebase found `origin/main` had, in the meantime, independently claimed ADR-157 for a concurrent, unrelated change (the sub_workflow nested request-port row directly above) — the same multi-session numbering collision this table's ADR-149/150/151 rows already document happening repeatedly. |
| [159](ADR-159-workflow-mid-run-cancellation.md) | GitHub issue #37: `WorkflowSupervisor` has `TerminationBound::max_rounds`/`deadline_ms`/`max_stalls`/`max_resets` — all DECLARED limits, checked only at round boundaries — but nothing lets a caller signal "stop this run now" from OUTSIDE, imperatively (a user closing a UI, an upstream request being aborted), mirroring MAF's `workflow_cancellation.py`. Does a design reusing this codebase's own already-established `std::stop_source`/`std::stop_token` idiom (ADR-017, `core/stream.hpp`) survive independent red-teaming? | **Proposed — implemented, independently red-teamed once (design draft before any code existed), the central mechanism adversarially verified by mutation, pending project-owner sign-off.** The red team confirmed the central mechanism sound (thread safety of `std::stop_source`/`std::stop_token` across threads is a standard-specified guarantee, not an assumption; the alternative of having `cancel()` acquire `run_mutex_` is actually broken, not just worse — it would have to wait for the very run it's trying to stop to release the mutex first) but found two real problems, both fixed before implementation: (1) MUST-FIX — a SECOND exhaustive `switch (workflow_status)` the first draft's own file list missed entirely: `workflow_as_executor_detail::status_tag()` (`rt/workflow_as_executor.hpp`) has no `default:` case, unlike `finish()`'s own switch in `workflow_supervisor.hpp` — this project builds under `-Wall -Wextra -Werror` on gcc/clang as a hard gate, so adding `workflow_status::cancelled` without a case there would have broken that build (MSVC alone would not have caught it); fixed, both switches updated together. (2) MUST-FIX (proof-plan) — the round-boundary proof needed this session's own established deterministic-liveness shape (mirroring ADR-157/issue #42 item 3's own W12), not a timing race, or it risked the exact false-negative class this session has now caught three times; fixed by proving it with a genuinely blocked body (`condition_variable`, bounded wait) where `cancel()` is called and CONFIRMED to have landed while the body is still blocked, before it is released. New `WorkflowSupervisor::cancel()`/`cancellation_token()` (a `std::stop_source cancel_source_` member, mirroring `multiplex_sink_`'s own "always valid, no null state" shape), `workflow_status::cancelled`, ONE new check in `execute()`'s round loop at the EXACT SAME point every other termination bound is already checked, and cooperative mid-call opt-out via a new `EffectContext::cancellation` field (`std::stop_token`, default-constructed = always safe to read even unwired, the same idiom `report_progress`/`agent_turn_sink` already establish). Implementation refinement over the original plan: rather than adding yet another parameter to `run_executor_job()`, `execute()`'s dispatch loop instead builds a per-delivery COPY of the executor's own `EffectContext` with `ctx.cancellation` set before passing it in — `EffectContext` already IS the per-call vehicle for exactly this kind of opt-in signal, mirroring how `capabilities`/`bound_capabilities` already ride along on `ctx` rather than as separate parameters. New `tests/test_rt_workflow_cancellation.cpp` (17 checks) proving: deterministic round-boundary cancellation (not a race); the round already in flight when `cancel()` lands still completes normally, its real output preserved, not truncated; a body that explicitly checks the token observes a cross-thread `cancel()` call and can act on it; a body that does NOT check the token still runs to its own natural completion (genuinely cooperative, never accidentally preemptive); a run that never calls `cancel()` is byte-for-byte unaffected; and `cancellation_token()` mirrors `cancel()`'s effect for an independently-held handle. **Adversarially verified by mutation**: temporarily short-circuited the round-loop check — 8 of 17 checks failed exactly as expected (every check whose correctness depends on cancellation actually taking effect), while checks independent of it kept passing; reverted, reran clean. Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC): the new target plus a full clean project rebuild, zero errors. Explicitly does NOT preempt an already-dispatched, non-cooperative executor body (the same characteristic every existing bound already has — for a wide fan-out round, or any round containing a `sub_workflow` dispatch, "next round boundary" can be an effectively unbounded wait, said plainly rather than left implicit); does NOT automatically fan out one outer `cancel()` call into every bound nested `sub_workflow` instance (a host that already holds the `shared_ptr<WorkflowSupervisor>` a `bind_sub_workflow()` call required in the first place can call `inner->cancel()` directly, TODAY, with zero new machinery — only AUTOMATIC propagation from a single outer call is deferred, a real follow-on reusing the `ScopedForwardedEventSink`-style dispatch-time-wiring pattern issue #42 item 3 established); does NOT take an extra checkpoint at the cancellation point (the existing per-round checkpoint hook already fires before cancellation is ever checked, so a cancelled run's last completed round is always already checkpointed for free); does NOT persist cancellation state through checkpoint/resume (matches `nesting_depth_`/`event_path_prefix_`'s own already-established non-persistence precedent). |
| [160](ADR-160-parallel-tool-batch-scheduler.md) | GitHub issue #43: ADR-158 shipped `ExclusivityGroup<Name>` as a declared policy tag with zero pipeline logic — `Parallelizable`/`ExclusivityGroup<Name>` are both read by no dispatch path. Does 006 §8 G4's parallel-batch scheduler itself — the thing that would ever read either tag and run calls concurrently — survive an independent red-team pass and real implementation? | **Proposed — corrected, implemented, and proven; awaiting project-owner judgment.** An independent red-team pass found the original design's high-level framing (sequential admission, only `invoke()` fans out, results append in emitted order) sound, but its concrete mechanics reopened three already-fixed hazard classes through fields and call sites the first draft never inventoried: (1) MUST-FIX — batch partitioning ignored `ToolDescriptor::captures_session_state`, so a session-state-capturing tool (like the real, shipped `ScheduleWakeupTool`) declared `Parallelizable` would have been fanned to a worker thread with none of ADR-028's protection; fixed by checking `captures_session_state` first, forcing such a call into its own sequential singleton class unconditionally. (2) MUST-FIX — `report_progress` was kept live across concurrently-running calls, but its target (`emit_run_event_for()`) mutates an unlocked `std::unordered_map` AND pushes into a channel type documented "Multiple PRODUCERS... unsupported"; fixed with a new `run_event_mutex_` guarding the method's entire body, restoring effectively-single-producer semantics and eliminating the map race in one stroke. (3) MUST-FIX — the `EffectContext` per-call-copy fix named only two of four reverse-channel fields, missing `agent_turn_sink`/`moderator_delta_sink` (ADR-152); resolved for the top-level case (a plain struct copy naturally preserves them), explicitly scoped OUT for a workflow-embedded nested session (a new non-goal, pending separate verification against ADR-152's own multiplexer). (4) MUST-FIX — the design's own inventory of dispatch loops undercounted the real call sites (two named, four actually exist); corrected, with the single-call codeact-ask replay site excluded by construction. (5) MUST-FIX — within-group serialization was unspecified, risking pool starvation; fixed by making the CLASS (not each call) the unit of fan-out — an `ExclusivityGroup<Name>` runs its members as an ordinary sequential loop inside ONE job/thread. (6) MUST-FIX — `bound_capabilities` lifetime across the admission-thread → worker-thread handoff was unspecified; fixed by making each job own its `bound` vector as a stable member. Three SHOULD-FIX findings also resolved: `rt::ThreadPool` rejected as the fan-out mechanism (its one-resume-then-fault semantics would abandon a genuinely-suspending job and leak capability tickets) in favor of a small, purpose-built, bounded pool of real blocking OS threads (`rt::run_jobs_bounded`, new file `rt/bounded_call_fanout.hpp`); `blob_sink` concurrent-write safety against `WorktreeObjectStore::put_blob` named as an implementation-time proof obligation, not solved; event-stream ordering clarified as monotonic-but-not-deterministic (013 §1), distinct from `history_`'s own append-order determinism (I5-load-bearing). Implementation: `ToolDescriptor::parallelizable` (new field, mirroring `exclusivity_group`'s own shape); `admit_call()`/`run_admitted_call()` extracted from `invoke_tool()` (steps 1/4-7/5 and 8/9/10 respectively, byte-for-byte the same logic, zero behavior change — verified by the full pre-existing suite passing unchanged before any new call site was wired) so the parallel path shares ONE admission/invoke implementation rather than a third, independently-drifting copy; `partition_batch()` (pure function, no I/O); `AgentSession::dispatch_tool_calls()` wired into all three real batch-dispatch call sites (`resolve_interaction()`'s approved branch, `finish_hook_processed_round()`, `run_rounds()`'s own loop), degrading to exactly today's sequential loop, byte for byte, whenever a batch isn't uniformly eligible (every real batch today, since no shipped tool yet declares either tag). New `tests/test_tool_batch_partition.cpp` (20 checks, pure logic) and `tests/test_tool_batch_parallel_dispatch.cpp` (3 claims, real `AgentSession` round-trips): two independent `Parallelizable` calls proven to run on different OS threads via a rendezvous; two `ExclusivityGroup<"db-write">` calls proven to never overlap (concurrent high-water mark never exceeds 1); 64 real round-trips where the first-emitted call is deliberately the slower one, with EVERY iteration's physical completion order observed to be the reverse of emitted order while `history_`'s appended order stayed correct every time — positively confirming real concurrency, not merely inferring it from a result that a sequential implementation would also produce trivially. A genuine test-fixture bug (not a scheduler bug) was found and fixed during this pass by direct execution: `AE_JSON_SCHEMA` requires every field present in args JSON regardless of C++ default values, so an initial `"{}"` payload silently failed schema parsing before any tool body ran — diagnosed by direct instrumentation (since removed), not by inspection. Full rebuild (MSVC 14.51, `/W4 /WX`): zero warnings, zero errors. Full existing suite (256 tests): 100% pass, 0 failures, run twice (once after the `invoke_tool()` refactor alone, once after all three call sites were rewired) — the sequential fallback every real batch takes today is confirmed byte-for-byte unchanged. One unrelated, pre-existing flaky test in a different subsystem (`test_content_durability_concurrency`) failed once under a run contaminated by a concurrent rebuild; confirmed not a regression by standalone reruns and a subsequent clean full-suite pass. Explicitly does NOT amend 006 §5's text (the all-or-nothing-vs-per-class-fan-out question stays open, a real RFC-amendment decision for the project owner); does NOT fix the concurrency-bound's ultimate source (`kParallelBatchWorkerCap = 4` is a working placeholder, not a host-configurable knob), cancellation, or the workflow-embedded case (all three named, open, deferred); does NOT implement the literal 10⁴-iteration G4 gate text (64 iterations is real, executed evidence at a smaller, CI-practical scale); has NOT been independently red-teamed a second time against the implementation itself — only the design was red-teamed. NOTE: drafted and implemented locally as "ADR-159", renumbered to ADR-160 after a rebase/fetch found `origin/main` had, in the meantime, independently merged an unrelated ADR-159 (`WorkflowSupervisor` mid-run cancellation, issue #37, directly above) — the same multi-session numbering collision this table's ADR-149/150/151 and ADR-157/158 rows already document happening repeatedly. |
| [162](ADR-162-workflow-as-chat-client-adapter.md) | GitHub issue #35: can a whole, already-initialized `WorkflowSupervisor` satisfy this codebase's `ChatClient` concept — mirroring MAF's `workflow.as_agent()` — without silently fabricating token usage, corrupting a paused human-in-the-loop interaction the moment it's composed with `AgentSession`'s own turn loop, or a detached-worker thread-boundary hazard, and does a design for it survive ten rounds of independent red-teaming? | **Proposed — implemented, ten independent red-team rounds against the design before any code existed (the tenth reporting no remaining MUST-FIX defect), all evidence executed and passing, pending project-owner sign-off.** The most-iterated finding across the ten rounds (rounds 6, 7, 8, then 9) was `Usage`: a fabricated zero silently defeats real `.token_budget()` enforcement (a genuine I8 violation, since `WorkflowSupervisor` has no usage-tracking mechanism at all to report honestly); a `chat()` made to fail closed on missing usage instead broke the adapter's own headline "direct caller" use case, because `chat()`'s signature has no way to distinguish a budget-reliant `AgentSession` binding from a caller who never needed one — round 8's own fix, itself a regression, discovered only because round 9 traced `AgentSession::run_model_call()`'s real DEFAULT (non-streaming) branch and found it silently reproduced the exact defect round 8 believed it had eliminated. **Resolved structurally, not with a fourth patch**: `WorkflowChatClient` does not implement `chat()` at all — `ChatClient` (`core/chat_client.hpp`) already makes it optional for precisely this reason. A direct caller drains `chat_stream()` and never sees a failure (usage stays honestly `nullopt`, never fabricated); an `AgentSession` binding still cannot complete a call, but via that session's own pre-existing, already-audited `chat()`-absent fallback (`detail::drain_streaming_response()`'s existing fail-closed check), not a bespoke rule invented here — removing code closed more ground than the three prior rounds' patches had, and is the first round of the ten whose fix reduced the design's own surface area. Three more independently-iterated MUST-FIX threads, each also traced to a real defect with file:line evidence before being fixed: (1) the ask/resume signal is `Custom`, never `ToolCall` (round 1) — a `ToolCall`-shaped encoding lets `AgentSession::tool_calls_of()` silently auto-resolve a paused interaction with a fabricated answer, deterministically, the moment this adapter is bound as an ordinary agent's backend; (2) the message-history envelope is an opaque `Custom` item built from this codebase's own existing `message_to_json()` codec, never a physical flattening of content items (round 4, then round 9's own follow-through after the first "fix" for this was ALSO found wrong) — neither real backend (`OpenAIChatClient`/`AnthropicChatClient`) reads `ContentItem::origin`, both key wire translation entirely on the outer `Message::role`, so a flattened message reaching either one produces a garbled or wire-protocol-invalid request; (3) the `EffectContext` crossing the detached-worker thread boundary needed real, complete sanitization (rounds 3, 6, 7, 8) — a hand-picked field list kept missing fields as the design grew, and a later bare whole-struct copy still forwarded raw borrowed pointers (`bound_capabilities`, `sandbox_fs`) a real, already-established codebase precedent (`tool_pipeline.hpp::background_task()`) resets, not trusts — fixed with a directly-testable `sanitize_for_detached_worker()` function, confirmed complete by a full field-by-field audit of `EffectContext` (round 8) rather than another partial list. New `include/agentengine/rt/workflow_as_chat_client.hpp` (`WorkflowChatClient`), additive `WorkflowSupervisor::open_interaction_asks()` (`workflow_supervisor.hpp`, an `OpenPort`-only sibling of the existing `open_interactions()` — no existing method's behavior changed), `tests/test_rt_workflow_as_chat_client.cpp` (T1-T9, all CORRECT), `examples/28_workflow_as_chat_client.cpp` (a real `request_port` round-trip through two genuine `chat_stream()` calls, `OK`). The "prove" phase itself, independent of all ten red-team rounds, caught two further real implementation bugs by the tests actually failing, not by review: a wrong assumption that `output_selection` naming multiple executors combines their outputs into one multi-item message (it does not — a plain last-one-wins assignment with no `fan_in` edge merging them), and an invalid `max_rounds=0` construction that triggered `workflow_status::invalid` rather than the intended `bound_max_rounds` — both found, diagnosed, and fixed before this ADR was written. Built and run for real (Debug/MSVC, Visual Studio 18 2026): the new test (55/55 checks) and example both pass; full workflow-family `ctest -R "workflow"`: 32/32 passed, zero regressions in the sibling ADR-150 adapter or the core supervisor. Explicitly does NOT let an `AgentSession` bound to this adapter complete a call in ANY configuration until `WorkflowSupervisor` exposes real, aggregate `Usage` tracking (unbuilt today) — the accepted cost of never fabricating a token count, disclosed as a real, unbuilt prerequisite rather than silently narrowed; does NOT solve the "sub-agent behind an intermediate `AgentSession`" or "outer session with `set_output_schema()` armed" compositions (no designed answer-routing mechanism for either); does NOT let `RecordingChatClient<WorkflowChatClient>` compile (that type gates on the stricter `LegacyChatClient`, which needs `chat()` — a real, accepted trade, and re-adding even a stub `chat()` would silently reintroduce the exact defect this ADR closes, since `AgentSession`'s own dispatch is a pure type-level check on whether `chat()` exists, never on what it does); does NOT expose a nested `sub_workflow` interaction's own real ask (an honestly-empty placeholder only, ADR-157's own recursive-walk residual, unbuilt); does NOT touch issue #33 or ADR-150/issue #36 (unrelated mechanisms). |
| [163](ADR-163-workflow-supervisor-usage-tracking.md) | ADR-162's own named blocker: `WorkflowSupervisor` had no usage-tracking mechanism at all, so an `AgentSession` bound to `WorkflowChatClient` could never complete a call (fabricating a token count was rejected as a genuine I8 violation across ADR-162's own rounds 6-9). Explicit project-owner direction: close this for real, going further than MAF's own `workflow.as_agent()` (which reports no usage from a wrapped workflow at all). Can real, cumulative `Usage` be tracked without widening any executor's own authority or treating model output as anything but data? | **Proposed — implemented, verified by real test execution, pending project-owner sign-off.** `AgentSession::run_usage()` (new, reset alongside `run_tokens_consumed_`, accumulated on every real model response — deliberately NOT `AgentResponse::usage`, which only reflects the final round of a multi-round tool-calling turn and would under-count every earlier one) feeds `agent_session_as_executor_body()`'s new `ExecutorOutcome::usage`, which `WorkflowSupervisor` folds into a new cumulative `total_usage_`/`usage()` accessor at both places `execute()` already folds a reply, plus a nested `sub_workflow`'s own total bubbled in retroactively via `OpenPort::usage` at resolution. Not run through the full ten-round adversarial process ADR-159/160 used (pure aggregation/plumbing, no new authority surface or thread-boundary crossing) — verified instead by the tests genuinely failing first: two real defects (`run_executor_job()` and `run_sub_workflow_job()`'s COMPLETED branch both silently dropped `outcome->usage`/`inner->usage()` when constructing `ExecuteReply`, despite every struct already having the field) were caught by T10/T11 reporting 0/0 instead of the scripted 100/50 tokens, not by code review, then fixed. `test_rt_workflow_as_chat_client` (rebuilt and rerun clean), `test_rt_agent_workflow_executor`, and `test_rt_agent_session` all clean; `examples/28_workflow_as_chat_client.cpp` rerun, `OK`, now reporting a real honest-zero `Usage` instead of ADR-162's `nullopt`. **Closes ADR-162 §5's headline "an `AgentSession` bound to this adapter cannot complete a call" disclosure IN FULL, not just for the common case** — `WorkflowChatClient::chat_stream()` sets `usage` unconditionally on every terminal push (never merely when there's something nonzero to report), so `AgentSession::run_model_call()`'s `chat()`-absent fallback (`drain_streaming_response()`, which fails closed only on a genuinely MISSING usage) never trips, for a wrapped graph with a real `agent`-kind node OR one that is entirely `function`-kind. New T12 composes an outer, real `AgentSession<WorkflowChatClient>` end to end for both shapes and proves both complete — catching and correcting an earlier draft of this same ADR that had reasoned (wrongly, and untested) that the all-`function`-kind case would still fail closed. The residual that remains is about what NUMBER is reported, never about whether the composition completes: an ordinary `function`-kind `ExecutorBody` holding its own `ChatClient` directly still reports nothing through this tracked path, and a nested `sub_workflow` suspended mid-run still contributes zero to the outer total until it resolves. `usage()` is LIVE (readable mid-run, matching `open_interactions()`'s own shape) and resets only on a fresh `run_workflow()`, never on `resume_workflow()`/`continue_workflow()`, matching `rounds_`'s own suspend/resume-persistent contract. |
